## Absurdist (absurdist)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Drama and film in which conventional plot, characterization and logic are ignored or distorted to convey the irrationality of existence and humanity’s isolation in a meaningless world, as in the post-war Theater of the Absurd.
    What it asks of an actor. Absurdist drama, Beckett above all, asks actors for exact control of timing, rhythm and timed silence, and in Waiting for Godot for the cross-talk and clown business of vaudeville and music hall.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Comic Timing, Physical Comedy) belongs to Beckett’s plays (Waiting for Godot’s cross-talk and clown business), not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: The study guide, from a search summary: Beckett 'places strong emphasis on the rhythmic qualities of his plays, and like a composer, he is conscious of the importance of precisely timed silence'; his plays put actors in very difficult positions, some unable to speak and others unable to move; one actress says the voice must concentrate on 'timing, rhythm and the musicality of the text'. Wikipedia, from a search summary, says Godot borrows from 'the cross-talk tradition of the vaudeville stage, the burlesque, the music hall, and the movies of Charlie Chaplin, Buster Keaton, Laurel and Hardy', with hat games, dropped trousers and similar business. The Northern Star blog says Godot owes much of its form and rhythm to vaudeville and clown acts. (from a search summary; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: All of the evidence is Beckett, mostly Godot. Ionesco, Pinter and absurdist film aren’t covered. The study guide’s PDF couldn’t be read as text, so the quotations come from a search engine’s summary of it, not from a direct reading. Nothing found covers 'holding comedy and despair together' as an acting demand in so many words. On review (October 10, 2026), evidence that’s all Beckett wasn’t enough to make it a requirement of the whole genre.
    How its scenes are played. Beckett’s drama asks for exact control of timing, rhythm and timed silence inside a world whose logic is distorted. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    The World’s Own Logic: A world with rules of its own, absurd, dreamlike or satirical, played wholeheartedly by them and never commented on. A self-tape in it is judged more on interpretation and execution, and calls on your imagination and physical expression. The breakdown: the world’s logic has to be found before it can be played as if it were sense.
    Comic Rhythm: Setup and payoff: cues picked up, pauses placed, the laugh landing where the timing puts it. A self-tape in it is judged more on responsiveness and execution, and calls on your adaptability and focus. Rehearsing on your feet: timing lives in the exchange and is found by playing it through.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatre_of_the_absurd (Wikipedia; also dictionary.com’s 'theater of the absurd'). Source for what it asks: State Theatre Company South Australia, 'Beckett Trip' study guide, https://statetheatrecompany.com.au/wp-content/uploads/2022/05/Beckett-study-guide.pdf; Wikipedia, 'Waiting for Godot' (pointer only), https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waiting_for_Godot; Northern Star, 'Beckett: Double acts, music hall and his relationship to theatre', https://northernstarcr.wordpress.com/2015/12/15/beckett-double-acts-music-hall-and-his-relationship-to-theatre/.
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.
  ways: Imagination (Its scenes call on it: The World’s Own Logic) | Physical Expression (Its scenes call on it: The World’s Own Logic) | Adaptability (Its scenes call on it: Comic Rhythm) | Focus (Its scenes call on it: Comic Rhythm)
  provenance: Its definition was taken from a search summary, and what it asks is held with low confidence.

## Action (action)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A film genre that predominantly features chase sequences, fights, shootouts, explosions and stunt work.
    What it asks of an actor. The action genre is defined by fights, chases, shootouts and stunt work.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Stage Combat (yours: 54.6 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: The definition names fights and stunt work as what the genre predominantly features.
    What the research says: 'Action film is a film genre that predominantly features chase sequences, fights, shootouts, explosions, and stunt work.' Actor preparation isn’t discussed. (read directly; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: Wikipedia was used only as a lead. Stunt work is often done by doubles, so the page doesn’t say the actor does it.
    How its scenes are played. Its chases are stunt work and its fights stage combat; between them, the scenes turn on who holds the frame and what the threat is. The research found nothing on how it’s played, so this is estimated from its conventions.
    Command Of The Frame: Status and threat carried in the body and the look more than the words: the person everyone in the room watches. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.
    Suspense Held: A threat kept alive under every line: what’s at stake never dropped, even in the quiet. A self-tape in it is judged more on consistency and interpretation, and calls on your focus and listening and responsiveness. The breakdown, then rehearsal: suspense is held by knowing at every moment what your character fears will happen, and keeping it alive through the scene.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Action_film (Wikipedia). Source for what it asks: Wikipedia, 'Action film', https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Action_film (pointer).
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.
  ways: Stage Combat (What it asks for: Fights And Stunts) | Presence (Its scenes call on it: Command Of The Frame) | Physical Expression (Its scenes call on it: Command Of The Frame) | Focus (Its scenes call on it: Suspense Held) | Listening And Responsiveness (Its scenes call on it: Suspense Held)
  provenance: Its definition was read at the source, and what it asks is held with medium confidence.

## Action Comedy (action_comedy)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A genre combining action and comedy, with fast action that stays mostly lighthearted and rarely involves death or serious injury.
    What it asks of an actor. Action comedy combines action with humor that plays a central role, descended from silent comedians’ chases, gags and stunts.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Stage Combat (yours: 54.6 of 90) , Comedy (yours: 57.7 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: The genre is action plus comedy; the page links its roots to chases and stunts. Humor 'plays a relatively central role' by definition.
    What the research says: Read through an automatic summary of the page: humor 'plays a relatively central role'; silent comedians such as Chaplin and Keaton featured 'chases, gags and stunts'. The earlier quoted phrase 'relies on the characters to bring out the humor' didn’t come back in this reading. (read directly; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: The page was read through an automatic summary, and its remarks on Jackie Chan may be the summary’s own, so the research didn’t name Physical Comedy.
    How its scenes are played. Humor plays a central role beside the action, descended from the silent comedians’ gags. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Comic Rhythm: Setup and payoff: cues picked up, pauses placed, the laugh landing where the timing puts it. A self-tape in it is judged more on responsiveness and execution, and calls on your adaptability and focus. Rehearsing on your feet: timing lives in the exchange and is found by playing it through.
    Command Of The Frame: Status and threat carried in the body and the look more than the words: the person everyone in the room watches. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Action_comedy (Wikipedia). Source for what it asks: Wikipedia, 'Action comedy', https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Action_comedy (pointer).
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.

## Adventure (adventure)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A film genre whose plots feature travel, typically sending protagonists away from home to distant or exotic places to fulfill a goal such as a quest or search.
    What it asks of an actor. A film reference work treats action and adventure cinema together as a form that foregrounds the body in motion and in combat, with a hero who must show physical endurance. In its swashbuckler strand the hero displays martial skill in extravagant swordplay, and stars such as Fairbanks did much of their own stunt work.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Stage Combat (yours: 54.6 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: Tasker’s encyclopedia entry says of the whole form that action and adventure cinema foregrounds the body in motion and in combat, which makes on-screen combat a convention of the genre. It’s one credible source, so the research holds it at medium confidence, not high. Stars doing their own stunts (Fairbanks, Flynn) is a matter for swashbuckler stars, and isn’t what the requirement rests on.
    What the research says: Action and Adventure Films: "as a form that foregrounds the body in motion and in combat, action and adventure cinema advances a physical (frequently sexualized), imagery of heroism"; "the action or adventure hero is called upon to demonstrate courage, initiative and physical endurance"; the swashbuckler hero is shown "displaying martial skills in extravagant scenes of swordplay"; Flynn’s "athletic performance came to define the romantic male exuberance of the swashbuckler." Douglas Fairbanks: No Stuntman Required: Fairbanks "completed most stunts himself" and secretly did the Robin Hood drawbridge stunt after agreeing to a double. It makes no claim about swashbuckler stars in general. Swordsmen of the Screen: From Douglas Fairbanks to Michael York: Quoted (via Wikipedia, not read directly) as saying that to understand a swashbuckler 'we must look at the art director, costume designer, fencing master, stunt arranger, cinematographer and actor'. The book’s existence and authorship are confirmed by the Wikipedia citation; the quote itself wasn’t seen on a page read directly. (two of its three sources read directly and the remaining one from a search summary, reviewed October 10, 2026; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: Tasker treats action and adventure as one form; scholars such as Brian Taves separate historical adventure from action, and some adventure films (travel or exploration stories) have little combat. Only one source says it of the whole genre directly. The Richards quote wasn’t read in the book itself.
    How its scenes are played. A reference work treats its hero as a body in motion who must show endurance and carry the quest. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Command Of The Frame: Status and threat carried in the body and the look more than the words: the person everyone in the room watches. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adventure_film (Wikipedia). Source for what it asks: Yvonne Tasker, Action and Adventure Films (Schirmer Encyclopedia of Film (ed. Barry Keith Grant), via Encyclopedia.com, 2007), https://www.encyclopedia.com/arts/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/action-and-adventure-films [read directly]; Wisconsin Center for Film and Theater Research (blog post), Douglas Fairbanks: No Stuntman Required (WCFTR, University of Wisconsin-Madison, 2013), https://wcftr.commarts.wisc.edu/index.php/2013/05/16/douglas-fairbanks-no-stuntman-required/ [read directly]; Jeffrey Richards, Swordsmen of the Screen: From Douglas Fairbanks to Michael York (Routledge & Kegan Paul (reissued Routledge 2014), 1977), https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swashbuckler_film [from a search summary].
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.
  ways: Stage Combat (What it asks for: Fights And Stunts) | Presence (Its scenes call on it: Command Of The Frame) | Physical Expression (Its scenes call on it: Command Of The Frame)
  provenance: Its definition was taken from a search summary, and what it asks is held with medium confidence.

## Alternate History (alternate_history)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A genre of fiction that imagines a different course of history following a changed key event or figure, and explores the consequences of that point of divergence.
    What it asks of an actor. No source was found that describes a performance demand particular to alternate history.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: Nothing usable. One page the search found (explore.st-aug.edu) was generic, unsourced text, and wasn’t used. (from a search summary; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: The genre is defined by its premise, not its performance, and its definition calls for none of the requirements the game can ask for. Anything a period asks would come from the period the story imitates, which the Costume Drama genre covers, not from this one.
    How its scenes are played. A past that didn’t happen: another age’s manners, and a world changed by one event, both to be made concrete. The research found nothing on how it’s played, so this is estimated from its conventions.
    Another Age’s Manners: How people of another time stood, moved, spoke and kept their place, kept up without looking like effort. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and interpretation, and calls on your physical expression, discipline and vocal expression. The breakdown and rehearsal together: the manners are researched first, then made habit on your feet.
    Belief In What Isn’t There: An invented world made concrete: the creature, the machine or the magic played as specifically as a cup of tea. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and interpretation, and calls on your imagination. The breakdown: the world’s rules have to be worked out before they can be believed.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://www.britannica.com/topic/alternate-history (Encyclopaedia Britannica). Source for what it asks: None found. A search for actors' approach on The Man in the High Castle returned only cast lists and low-quality pages.
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.
  ways: Physical Expression (Its scenes call on it: Another Age’s Manners) | Discipline (Its scenes call on it: Another Age’s Manners) | Vocal Expression (Its scenes call on it: Another Age’s Manners) | Imagination (Its scenes call on it: Belief In What Isn’t There)
  provenance: Its definition was taken from a search summary, and what it asks is held with low confidence.

## Animated (animated)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Films made from a series of drawings, computer graphics or photographed objects such as puppets or models that, viewed in quick succession, create the appearance of movement.
    What it asks of an actor. Actors in animation perform with the voice alone, usually alone in a recording booth and often before the animation exists, putting a full physical performance into what is only heard.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Voice-Over (yours: 54.0 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: The sources describe the animation actor’s job as voice-only performance in a booth, often without a scene partner.
    What the research says: From a search summary: 'Voice actors may or may not have a scene partner while recording, so they practice delivering their dialogue solo'; voice actors 'stand alone in a soundproof room while delivering a full-body performance that audiences will only ever hear'; the best animation voice actors 'gesture, crouch, jump, and grimace while recording'. A further result (a TikTok video, not used as a source) described prelay, recording before animation. The summary merged several pages, so which page holds which sentence wasn’t checked. (from a search summary; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: Neither page was read directly. The claim is widely repeated, but repetition wasn’t counted as extra support. Stop-motion and motion-capture work differ and aren’t covered.
    How its scenes are played. The actor puts a full physical performance into a voice alone, often before the picture exists. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Played Large: Feeling worn on the outside and sized to fill the frame: the voice and the body carry it as much as the face. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and connection, and calls on your vocal expression, physical expression and presence. Rehearsing on your feet: a size the body and the voice can carry has to be found aloud and standing.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://www.britannica.com/dictionary/animation (Britannica Dictionary). Source for what it asks: Backstage, 'How to Become a Cartoon Voice Actor', https://www.backstage.com/magazine/article/how-to-become-a-voice-actor-cartoons-75311/; Real Voice Talent, 'Voice Acting for Animation: Different Skills Required for Character Voices', https://www.realvotalent.com/blog/voice-acting-for-animation-different-skills-required-for-character-voices.
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.
  ways: Voice-Over (What it asks for: Voice-Led Performance) | Vocal Expression (Its scenes call on it: Played Large) | Physical Expression (Its scenes call on it: Played Large) | Presence (Its scenes call on it: Played Large)

## Anime (anime)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A style of animation originating in Japan, used in Japanese films and television programs, known for colorful images, strong characters and action-heavy stories.
    What it asks of an actor. Dubbing anime asks the actor to match timing to mouth movements that already exist, keeping the performance emotionally true while it fits the lip flaps.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Voice-Over (yours: 54.0 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: Both sources describe voice-only performance synced to existing animation.
    What the research says: D.C. Douglas (read directly) quotes voice actors. Crispin Freeman: 'It’s like a dance where timing is everything. Practicing with the lip flaps in silent mode can help you get the timing down.' Cherami Leigh: 'focus on matching the mouth movements precisely.' Mary Elizabeth McGlynn: 'You need to feel what the character feels.' Mix (from a search summary): the hardest problem for dubbing directors is 'getting natural performances from actors who are trying to match the rhythms of pre-existing mouth flaps'. (read directly; high confidence).
    Still uncertain: This covers English-language dubbing only. The original Japanese voice performance (seiyuu work) wasn’t researched. A 'heightened, expressive register' isn’t supported by anything found.
    How its scenes are played. Dubbing asks for emotional truth sized to the drawn image while the timing fits mouth movements that already exist. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Played Large: Feeling worn on the outside and sized to fill the frame: the voice and the body carry it as much as the face. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and connection, and calls on your vocal expression, physical expression and presence. Rehearsing on your feet: a size the body and the voice can carry has to be found aloud and standing.
    Dense Text, Exact: A lot of words, or difficult ones, delivered fluently and at speed: jargon, argument or deduction as second nature. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and interpretation, and calls on your memory and focus. Learning the lines: fluency at speed is the lines learned past knowing them.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://www.britannica.com/dictionary/anime (Britannica Dictionary); https://www.britannica.com/art/anime-Japanese-animation (Encyclopaedia Britannica). Source for what it asks: D.C. Douglas, 'Tips For ADR Voice Acting in Anime Dubbing', https://dcdouglas.com/voiceover-resources/adr-dubbing-voice-acting/; Mix, 'The Art of Anime Dubbing', https://www.mixonline.com/sfp/art-anime-dubbing-369119.
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.
  ways: Voice-Over (What it asks for: Voice-Led Performance) | Vocal Expression (Its scenes call on it: Played Large) | Physical Expression (Its scenes call on it: Played Large) | Presence (Its scenes call on it: Played Large) | Memory (Its scenes call on it: Dense Text, Exact) | Focus (Its scenes call on it: Dense Text, Exact)
  provenance: Its definition was taken from a search summary, and what it asks is held with high confidence.

## Anthology (anthology)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A single film or series made of several self-contained shorter stories, often by different directors or writers, tied together by a shared theme, premise or framing device.
    What it asks of an actor. Some anthology films cast one actor in several roles across their stories, but no source treats this as a convention of the form.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: From a search summary, examples only: Cloud Atlas, where the cast play multiple roles across eras; Gene Kelly in different characters in each story of Invitation to the Dance; Mel Brooks in four roles in History of the World, Part I. (from a search summary; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: These are examples, not a convention, and nothing found supports 'establishing a character quickly in a short segment'. Cloud Atlas also shows heavy makeup transformation, but that’s one film, so the research doesn’t name Physical Transformation for the genre.
    How its scenes are played. A form, not a style: each story is a drama, played truthfully in a few minutes. The research found nothing on how it’s played, so this is estimated from its conventions.
    Played Straight: Meant completely: the feeling played with full conviction, never winked at, however large or strange the material. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and connection, and calls on your emotional depth and presence. The breakdown and the lines: believing it starts with knowing exactly what your character wants and why it matters to them.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthology_film (Wikipedia). Source for what it asks: Wikipedia, 'List of actors who have played multiple roles in the same film' (pointer only), https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_actors_who_have_played_multiple_roles_in_the_same_film; Collider, '13 Best Movies With Actors Playing Multiple Roles', https://collider.com/best-movies-actors-play-multiple-roles-austin-powers/.
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.
  ways: Emotional Depth (Its scenes call on it: Played Straight) | Presence (Its scenes call on it: Played Straight)
  provenance: Its definition was taken from a search summary, and what it asks is held with low confidence.

## Apocalyptic (apocalyptic)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A subgenre of science fiction concerned with the end of civilization through nuclear war, plague, natural disaster or another existential catastrophe.
    What it asks of an actor. Only a single example was found: on The Road, the lead lost about 30 pounds and lived rough to play a starving survivor.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: From a search summary: Mortensen lost around 30 pounds by eating less; he 'literally became weaker'; he said the weight loss was the hardest part; some of the cast went days without showering. (from a search summary; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: This is one film. Physical transformation is shown there but not established as a convention of the genre, so the research doesn’t name Physical Transformation. 'Sustained fear, grief and desperation played truthfully' isn’t supported by any source found.
    How its scenes are played. The one source found is a lead who starved himself and lived rough to play survival at the end of the world. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    States At The Limit: Fear, grief or exhaustion at their extreme, sustained, and believed though the threat is often not in the room. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and execution, and calls on your emotional depth and physical expression. The lines, then rehearsal on your feet: an extreme state has to be found in the body, on lines you no longer think about.

## Art House (art_house)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Films made primarily as serious artistic expression, not for commercial success, often experimental and aimed at a niche, not a mass audience.
    What it asks of an actor. In the modernist strand of art cinema (Bresson, Antonioni), directors hold actors’ physical and vocal expressivity so far down that framing, editing and sound carry the characters’ inner life. Bresson called his performers 'models', not actors, and repeated lines until the 'acting' was gone.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Naturalism On Camera) belongs to the methods of particular modernist and auteur directors (Bresson, Antonioni) and of slow cinema, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Acting: In modernist films by Bresson and Antonioni, 'actors' use of their physical and vocal expressivity is so delimited by the directors that glimpses of their characters' inner experiences often are more clearly conveyed by the directors' framing, editing, sound, and production design choices.' Also: 'The scale and quality of actors' physical and vocal expressions are also keyed to the film’s style or genre.' Art Cinema: It says nothing about an acting method for art cinema as a whole; only that 'art films tended to delve into character psychology and sensibility, to investigate the drama of the interior'. This is evidence against an acting convention for the whole genre. "Notes" on Notes on the Cinematographer: The Bressonian 'model' differs from an actor and 'encompasses an attitude ... which goes beyond performance'; repetition of lines is Bresson’s way of 'recovering the automatism of real life' (quoting Bresson, Notes on the Cinematographer, p. 59). The Art Cinema as a Mode of Film Practice: Art cinema motivates its narratives by realism and authorial expressivity and uses 'realistic', psychologically complex characters. It describes characters and narration, not an acting technique. (three of its four sources read directly and the remaining one from a search summary, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: Art house is a market and exhibition category covering many styles. Bresson’s book wasn’t read directly, only quoted through Totaro. Naturalism On Camera is only an approximation: Bresson’s models are closer to the absence of performance than to naturalistic screen acting.
    How its scenes are played. In art cinema’s modernist strand directors hold expressivity so far down that framing and sound carry the inner life. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Feeling Held In: The feeling is there and kept under: stillness, economy, what isn’t said carrying what is. A self-tape in it is judged more on consistency and believability, and calls on your composure and focus. Learning the lines and rehearsing on your feet: control comes from knowing it well enough to hold it still.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

Source for what it asks: Cynthia Baron, Acting (Schirmer Encyclopedia of Film, ed. Barry Keith Grant (Schirmer Reference/Thomson Gale), via Encyclopedia.com, 2007), https://www.encyclopedia.com/arts/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/acting [read directly]; Tom Ryall, Art Cinema (Schirmer Encyclopedia of Film, via Encyclopedia.com, 2007), https://www.encyclopedia.com/arts/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/art-cinema [read directly]; Donato Totaro, "Notes" on Notes on the Cinematographer (Offscreen, 2004), https://offscreen.com/view/bresson_notes [read directly]; David Bordwell, The Art Cinema as a Mode of Film Practice (Film Criticism 4:1, 1979), http://hybridstyle.com/wp-content/uploads/2015/04/Bordwell-The-Art-Cinema-as-a-Mode-of-Film-Practice.pdf [from a search summary].

## Biker (biker)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.

    Its conventions. A film genre, regarded as a branch of exploitation film, portraying motorcycle-riding rebels who usually belong to an outlaw motorcycle club.
    What it asks of an actor. On The Wild Angels (1966), the film usually credited with starting the cycle, Corman required the professional actors to do their own motorcycle riding, and a lead who couldn’t ride was replaced.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Fights And Stunts) belongs to productions that ask actors to ride, as The Wild Angels did, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: From a search summary: 'Corman insisted that all the professional actors had to ride the bikes themselves because he wanted to be as authentic as possible. The principals are clearly seen doing all their own riding'. Also: 'since Chakiris didn’t know how to ride a motorcycle, however, and refused to do the film without doubles and stuntmen, Corman then offered the role to Peter Fonda'. The TCM page couldn’t be opened. (from a search summary; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: This is one film’s casting rule, not a documented convention of the whole cycle. Riding fits Fights And Stunts only loosely, since none of the requirements the game can ask for is driving or riding. On review (October 10, 2026), one film’s casting rule wasn’t enough to make it a requirement of the whole genre.
    How its scenes are played. The outlaw club runs on status and loyalty: who wears the patch, and who answers to whom. The research found nothing on how it’s played, so this is estimated from its conventions.
    Command Of The Frame: Status and threat carried in the body and the look more than the words: the person everyone in the room watches. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.

A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your presence, listening and responsiveness and emotional depth. Rehearsing on your feet: a partnership is built in the exchange, played through until each part answers the other.

## Biographical (biographical)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A film that dramatizes the life, or the defining period of the life, of a real person or group of people under their real names.
    What it asks of an actor. Playing a real person means working within documented fact: researching the subject and reproducing their voice, accent, mannerisms and movement, while trying to portray rather than impersonate.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Character Work (yours: 52.7 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: The source describes reproducing a real person’s movement, physicality and mannerisms (Chaplin, Lincoln).
    What the research says: Sabato (read directly): 'There is a fine line between portraying a person and impersonating a person.' Streep approached Thatcher’s voice like learning a song and worked with voice coaches on accent; Downey watched all of Chaplin’s films to reproduce his movement; Day-Lewis read over a hundred books on Lincoln to understand his physicality and speech. Biographical roles require working 'within the confines ... of an actual person’s existence'. Casting Networks (from a search summary): actors study footage, 'listening to their voice and observing their physicality'. (read directly; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: Film Inquiry is an independent film-criticism site, not a primary source. The examples are celebrated star performances, and not every biopic asks this much. The review of October 10, 2026 took Accents away: an accent is asked only where the subject spoke differently from the actor, which belongs to the role, not the genre.
    How its scenes are played. Playing a real person means documented fact: their voice, manners and movement reproduced, portrayed rather than impersonated. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Another Age’s Manners: How people of another time stood, moved, spoke and kept their place, kept up without looking like effort. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and interpretation, and calls on your physical expression, discipline and vocal expression. The breakdown and rehearsal together: the manners are researched first, then made habit on your feet.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.

## Black Comedy (black_comedy)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Comedy that juxtaposes morbid or ghastly elements with comic ones to underscore the senselessness or futility of life.
    What it asks of an actor. Reference works define black comedy as comedy that sets morbid or ghastly material against the comic, often through farce and low comedy. No credible source found states a specific acting technique (such as deadpan) for the genre; deadpan advice exists only for comedy in general.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research holds this (Comic Timing) with low confidence, so the game doesn’t treat it as a requirement of the genre. The small print below says why.
    What the research says: Black humor: Black humor is writing that juxtaposes morbid or ghastly elements with comical ones; it 'often uses farce and low comedy'. (The page couldn’t be opened, so this is from a search summary.) It defines the genre as comic and says nothing about acting. Black comedy: It treats black comedy as a form of comedy with film examples (Dr. Strangelove, Harold and Maude, Fargo, The Death of Stalin). It doesn’t discuss performance, deadpan or timing. Deadpan: It advises sincere commitment to absurd situations ('The more absurd the situation becomes, the more useful sincere commitment can be'). It doesn’t mention black or dark comedy. (two of its three sources read directly and the remaining one from a search summary, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: Britannica couldn’t be opened, so it was read only as a search summary. No acting or training source ties a particular comic technique to black comedy: the idea of deadpan, of playing it straight, appears only in general comedy advice and in blogs that aren’t credible. It holds for the whole genre only by definition, and no source states a technical convention, so it stays at low confidence.
    How its scenes are played. Morbid material set against the comic, as farce does it: the horror meant, the timing exact. The research found nothing on how it’s played, so this is estimated from its conventions.
    Played Straight: Meant completely: the feeling played with full conviction, never winked at, however large or strange the material. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and connection, and calls on your emotional depth and presence. The breakdown and the lines: believing it starts with knowing exactly what your character wants and why it matters to them.
    Comic Rhythm: Setup and payoff: cues picked up, pauses placed, the laugh landing where the timing puts it. A self-tape in it is judged more on responsiveness and execution, and calls on your adaptability and focus. Rehearsing on your feet: timing lives in the exchange and is found by playing it through.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

## Blaxploitation (blaxploitation)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A 1970s subgenre of American exploitation film featuring Black protagonists, made for African American urban audiences and often dealing with crime, drugs, violence and racial conflict.
    What it asks of an actor. Scholarship characterizes the blaxploitation movement by, among other things, excessive violence, and describes its black action cinema as heroes, male and female, deploying violence, guns and martial arts. Stars doing their own stunts is attested only for individuals such as Pam Grier.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research holds this (Fights And Stunts) with low confidence, so the game doesn’t treat it as a requirement of the genre. The small print below says why.
    What the research says: Blaxploitation Films of the 1970s: Blackness and Genre: Publisher description: the movement "is characterized by films that feature a black hero or heroine, black supporting characters, a predominately black urban setting, a display of black sexuality, excessive violence, and a contemporary rhythm and blues soundtrack." Also: "Blaxploitation films were made across varying genres" (detective, horror, gangster, cop action). Action and Adventure Films: "The 1970s also saw the emergence of black action cinema (sometimes called 'blaxploitation') with both male and female heroes deploying violence, gun power, and martial arts against oppressive enemies and institutions." Karate, Kung Fu and Blaxploitation: "The 1970's marked the beginning of the one-two combination of Blaxploitation and Kung Fu"; Jim Kelly’s "superb karate skills". It doesn’t say how common fights were across the cycle, or whether stars did their own. (all three of its sources read directly, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: Lawrence’s 'excessive violence' comes from the publisher’s description of his book, not a page of the book itself. Violence includes gunplay, which asks less of the actor than hand-to-hand fights. Horror entries such as Blacula fit the cycle but aren’t action films. 'Larger-than-life star presence' still has no source. Fights And Stunts is inferred from the cycle’s violence, and no source states fight work for the genre as a whole, so it stays at low confidence.
    How its scenes are played. Scholarship describes its heroes as commanding, violent stars of urban action. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Command Of The Frame: Status and threat carried in the body and the look more than the words: the person everyone in the room watches. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://researchguides.dartmouth.edu/filmgenres/blaxploitation (Dartmouth College film genres research guide); https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/blaxploitation. Source for what it asks: Novotny Lawrence, Blaxploitation Films of the 1970s: Blackness and Genre (Routledge, 2008), https://www.routledge.com/Blaxploitation-Films-of-the-1970s-Blackness-and-Genre/Lawrence/p/book/9780415540957 [read directly]; Yvonne Tasker, Action and Adventure Films (Schirmer Encyclopedia of Film, via Encyclopedia.com, 2007), https://www.encyclopedia.com/arts/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/action-and-adventure-films [read directly]; Museum of UnCut Funk, Karate, Kung Fu and Blaxploitation (Google Arts & Culture, n.d.), https://artsandculture.google.com/story/karate-kung-fu-and-blaxploitation-museum-of-uncut-funk/rQXRbvxKvFtImw?hl=en [read directly].

## Body Horror (body_horror)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A subgenre of horror that showcases grotesque or disturbing violations of the human body, such as mutation, disease, mutilation or uncontrolled transformation.
    What it asks of an actor. Body horror built on practical effects puts its actors in heavy prosthetics for long makeup sessions, at real physical cost. The Substance had five- to eight-hour applications.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Physical Transformation) belongs to practical-effects roles in heavy prosthetics, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: From a search summary: full-body makeup took seven hours, more complex applications five to five and a half; Demi Moore or Margaret Qualley spent six to eight hours in makeup on the heaviest days; the production chose practical effects 'like those out of The Fly'. CNN: Qualley’s skin took about a year to recover from the prosthetics. (from a search summary; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: There’s one film, plus a reference to The Fly. Body horror done with digital effects wouldn’t ask this, so Physical Transformation rests on practical-effects practice. 'Conveying pain and loss of bodily control' isn’t supported by these sources. On review (October 10, 2026), one film’s practical-effects prosthetics weren’t enough to make it a requirement of the whole genre.
    How its scenes are played. Its actors perform through prosthetics at real physical cost, in states of bodily violation. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    States At The Limit: Fear, grief or exhaustion at their extreme, sustained, and believed though the threat is often not in the room. ## Buddy Comedy (buddy_comedy)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.

    Its conventions. A subgenre in which two people, usually with contrasting personalities, go on an adventure, mission or trip and build a friendship through comic friction.
    What it asks of an actor. The buddy comedy is a comedy subgenre built on two contrasting leads, and its formula gives the actors room to show chemistry and comic timing. Comedy teams such as Laurel and Hardy and Hope and Crosby are part of its lineage.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research holds this (Comic Timing) with low confidence, so the game doesn’t treat it as a requirement of the genre. The small print below says why.
    What the research says: The Best Buddy Cop Movies of All Time: The buddy comedy formula involves two opposites coming together to fight crime and often each other, 'providing the actors playing them plenty of room to demonstrate chemistry and comedic timing'. The page couldn’t be opened, so this is from a search summary. The page couldn’t be opened, so this is from a search summary. Comedy: It discusses comedy teams, such as Laurel and Hardy’s 'tit-for-tat exchanges of comic violence' and the 'inspired teaming' of Hope and Crosby in the Road pictures. It doesn’t state an acting demand for buddy films specifically. (one of its three sources read directly and the other two from search summaries, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).

## Buddy Cop (buddy_cop)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it

    Its conventions. A film and television genre in which two people of very different personalities, usually police officers or agents, are forced to work together to solve a crime.
    What it asks of an actor. Academic studies describe the buddy-cop film as an action-comedy hybrid: known for action and best known for its comedy, with witty banter, snappy one-liners and over-the-top stunts as hallmarks.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research holds this (Comic Timing, Fights And Stunts) with low confidence, so the game doesn’t treat it as a requirement of the genre. The small print below says why.
    What the research says: Why Can’t We Be Friends: The Legitimization of Police Violence in the Buddy-Cop Film Genre: 'the witty banter, the over the top stunts, and the fast pace action that are hallmarks of the genre'; 'The action-comedy relies on the witty comebacks and snappy one-liners of the characters in the film to cut the tension in violent' scenes; Lethal Weapon 'features big explosions, car chases, extreme stunt-work'. On and Off Screen: Race in Buddy Cop Films: 'Known for its comradery, action, and comedy, this genre...'; 'The buddy cop genre, aside from action, is known best for its use of comedy.' (both its sources read directly, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).

Neither speaks about what actors train for. The Rush Hour split of stunts and comedy between partners remains one franchise, not a convention. Comedy-led entries such as Ride Along may lean less on combat. Student works don’t establish a convention, so it stays at low confidence.
    How its scenes are played. An action-comedy hybrid known for its banter and its two mismatched partners. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.

## Chanbara (chanbara)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. The Japanese ‘sword-fighting’ film genre, a sub-category of jidaigeki (period drama) centered on samurai and their swordplay.
    What it asks of an actor. Chanbara is by name the sword-fighting film, and its actors perform tate: choreographed stage and screen sword-fighting built from memorized fixed forms.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Stage Combat (yours: 54.6 of 90) . This follows from how the genre is defined, not from a source that says so. A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: The genre is defined by swordfights, and the tate source says actors memorize and perform the fight forms.
    What the research says: From a search summary: chanbara means 'sword fighting' films, from the onomatopoeia 'chan-chan-bara-bara', and is a sub-category of jidaigeki. Tate is 'stage fighting in brawls or with swords, in stage plays and movies' whose 'movements come in fixed forms, which are memorized and combined by many actors to create a flashy sword fight'. The Shinkokugeki troupe was known for realistic tate. (from a search summary; high confidence).
    Still uncertain: The pages weren’t read directly, but the genre’s own name calls for it. Period movement, stillness and period speech aren’t supported by these sources, so the research didn’t name Heightened Language.
    How its scenes are played. Its actors perform tate, fixed sword forms, with the posture and etiquette of the samurai’s age. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Another Age’s Manners: How people of another time stood, moved, spoke and kept their place, kept up without looking like effort. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and interpretation, and calls on your physical expression, discipline and vocal expression. The breakdown and rehearsal together: the manners are researched first, then made habit on your feet.
    Command Of The Frame: Status and threat carried in the body and the look more than the words: the person everyone in the room watches. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.

## Chase (chase)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Films in which a chase or race is the primary plot element, a form dating to early cinema and its comic chase films.
    What it asks of an actor. Reference works define the chase film as an early and silent-era form in which a chase is the primary element, built on editing and on crowds running from one place to the next; its comic branch, from music-hall chase skits to Sennett’s Keystone chases, is physical comedy. Nothing credible makes stunts or fight work a convention of the form.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research holds this (Fights And Stunts) with low confidence, so the game doesn’t treat it as a requirement of the genre. The small print below says why.
    What the research says: Early Cinema: In chase films "large groups of people run from one locale to the next, the cut introducing a new setting while sustaining the sense of frantic movement"; the effect rests on "the accelerated sensation of displacement and mobility editing helps to promote." Chase film (entry in A Dictionary of Film Studies): From a search summary, as the page couldn’t be opened: chase films are films of the early and silent eras in which a chase is the primary element, with precedent in music-hall and vaudeville comedy chase skits 'punctuated with pratfalls and physical comedy'. Slapstick Comedy: Sennett realized "the comic potential of the chase"; his films ended with Kops and others who "careen across the screen, fall over, collide, and generally create mayhem." (two of its three sources read directly and the remaining one from a search summary, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: The Oxford entry was seen only as a search summary. Modern car-chase action films are usually classed as action films, not as 'chase films', and their driving is mostly done by stunt drivers.
    How its scenes are played. A chase is the plot: the pursuer always closing, the pursued at the end of their strength. The research found nothing on how it’s played, so this is estimated from its conventions.
    Suspense Held: A threat kept alive under every line: what’s at stake never dropped, even in the quiet. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and execution, and calls on your emotional depth and physical expression. The lines, then rehearsal on your feet: an extreme state has to be found in the body, on lines you no longer think about.

    What it asks of an actor. Comic timing, the use of pauses, cadence, delivery and pacing to land a joke, is the core comic skill.

## Coming Of Age (coming_of_age)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.

    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read

    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Stories that follow a young protagonist through the transition from childhood to adulthood, focused on growth and the loss of innocence.
    What it asks of an actor. Several noted coming-of-age films are praised for naturalistic performances, some from nonprofessional young actors with improvised dialogue, but no source treats this as a convention of the genre.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: From a search summary: The 400 Blows has a 'wonderfully naturalistic performance' from Jean-Pierre Léaud; The Florida Project cast nonprofessional children, and 'improvised actions and dialogue between the kids' are cited as naturalist traits; The Breakfast Club’s library confession scene was 'mostly improvised'. (from a search summary; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: These are examples, not a convention. Improvisation and Naturalism On Camera were considered and left out, because the sources don’t tie them to the genre.
    How its scenes are played. Noted films are praised for naturalistic, sometimes improvised performances of a young person’s first losses. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Caught Live: Behavior that looks unrehearsed: real listening, the next line taken from what the other person just did. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your listening and responsiveness and adaptability. Rehearsing on your feet: what looks unrehearsed is found by playing it through until nothing is set but the wants.
    Played Straight: Meant completely: the feeling played with full conviction, never winked at, however large or strange the material. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and connection, and calls on your emotional depth and presence. The breakdown and the lines: believing it starts with knowing exactly what your character wants and why it matters to them.

## Concert Film (concert_film)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A film whose content consists primarily of footage of an extended live music performance before an audience.
    What it asks of an actor. A concert film records an extended live performance by musicians (bands, singers) or sometimes stand-up comedians, before an audience. Its performers are playing themselves live, not acting roles, and singing is common but not universal.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Singing) belongs to concert films of singers and vocal acts, where the performers sing as themselves, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Docs That Really Rock: Music Documentaries Go Beyond the Performance: The concert/performance film, with Woodstock (1970) as 'the basic template', may include interstitial sequences 'but it’s primarily about watching a performance'. Jonathan Demme: it documents 'a musical event that happened and was attended by ticket-buying people'. Concert film: Used only as a lead, it defines the subject as an extended live music performance; other glossaries add stand-up comedians. (one of its two sources read directly and the other from a search summary, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: No scholarly reference work defining the genre was read: the IDA article is a trade source. None of the requirements the game can ask for is playing an instrument or live music, so Singing is a stretch. Whether an acted character belongs in a concert film at all is a question for the game, not the research.
    How its scenes are played. Its performers play themselves live before an audience, sized to the hall. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Played Large: Feeling worn on the outside and sized to fill the frame: the voice and the body carry it as much as the face. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and connection, and calls on your vocal expression, physical expression and presence. Rehearsing on your feet: a size the body and the voice can carry has to be found aloud and standing.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

## Cosmic Horror (cosmic_horror)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.

    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read

    Its conventions. A subgenre of horror, also called Lovecraftian horror, built on dread of the unknowable and humanity’s insignificance before vast, indifferent forces, favoring psychological horror over gore.
    What it asks of an actor. Lovecraftian stories typically end with the protagonist’s sanity cracking, and Backstage advises actors to track that descent and often play it as quiet detachment rather than ranting.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: From a search summary, Backstage: 'madness takes many forms and isn’t always ranting and raving ... it’s important to carefully track the role’s emotional journey and find ways to subtly portray a detachment from reality.' Wikipedia: a staple ending is that the protagonist 'perceives the unexplainable horror and the enormity of it cracks their sanity'. The Backstage page couldn’t be opened. (from a search summary; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: None of the requirements the game can ask for fits a portrayal of a mind unraveling. Reacting to what can’t be shown isn’t supported by these sources: see the Creature Feature genre for the evidence on reacting to nothing.
    How its scenes are played. Backstage advises playing the descent into madness as quiet detachment, not ranting. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.

A self-tape in it is judged more on consistency and believability, and calls on your composure and focus. Learning the lines and rehearsing on your feet: control comes from knowing it well enough to hold it still.
    States At The Limit: Fear, grief or exhaustion at their extreme, sustained, and believed though the threat is often not in the room. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and execution, and calls on your emotional depth and physical expression. The lines, then rehearsal on your feet: an extreme state has to be found in the body, on lines you no longer think about.

## Costume Drama (costume_drama)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A film or television drama set in the past in which the actors wear the costumes of a former age.
    What it asks of an actor. Period drama asks actors to learn period movement and behavior (deportment, greetings, bows, sitting and walking in period clothes, period dances) and sometimes period speech, and conservatories teach it as its own discipline.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: RADA (read directly): the course examines 'deportment, greetings, social interaction, clothing, and dance' and 'period-specific movement, style and codes of behaviour' from the Elizabethan and Jacobean courts to the 1920s. A search summary of the other results: 'A corset ... changes how you breathe, how you sit, how you bend'; actors consult 'a speech and voice teacher, as well as a movement teacher, to find the most specific tempos, rhythms, pronunciations, and specific physical movements that are truest to the period'. Which page carries the speech-teacher sentence wasn’t checked. (read directly; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: RADA’s course is evidence that period movement is a taught skill. It doesn’t say every costume drama demands it. Period dance appears in RADA’s syllabus, but the research didn’t name Dancing, because no source says costume dramas require it. Heightened Language, which is about language, fit this movement only loosely, and the review of October 10, 2026 took it away: it’s verse and rhetoric, and the research is about period movement and deportment, which none of the requirements the game can ask for covers.
    How its scenes are played. Actors learn period movement and behavior, deportment, greetings and bows, and sometimes period speech. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Another Age’s Manners: How people of another time stood, moved, spoke and kept their place, kept up without looking like effort. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and interpretation, and calls on your physical expression, discipline and vocal expression. The breakdown and rehearsal together: the manners are researched first, then made habit on your feet.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/costume%20drama (Merriam-Webster); https://www.collinsdictionary.com/dictionary/english/costume-drama. Source for what it asks: RADA, 'Period Movement for Actors', https://www.rada.ac.uk/short-courses/period-movement-actors/; HB Studio, 'Period Style', https://www.hbstudio.org/classes/physical-acting-techniques/period-style/; Backstage, 'What is a Period Piece?', https://www.backstage.com/magazine/article/how-to-approach-acting-in-a-period-piece-72139/.
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.

## Creature Feature (creature_feature)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A subgenre of horror and science fiction in which a monster or creature serves as the primary antagonist to humans.
    What it asks of an actor. Creature films with practical creatures have used a recognized specialty, the creature or prosthetic performer, who acts head to toe in suits and prosthetics with restricted sight and hearing. That demand belongs to the creature role, not to the human cast.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Physical Transformation) belongs to the creature performer role in practical-effects (suit or prosthetic) creature films, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Doug Jones, Unmasked: Jones: 'I’m one of the top "makeup actors" in the movie business, appearing head to toe in prosthetic pieces and elaborate costumes.' The Shape Of Water And Pan’s Labyrinth Were Very Different Prosthetics Experiences For Doug Jones: Jones: 'Any creature suit that I’ve worn over the last 31 years hampers your eyesight, your hearing'; Pan’s Labyrinth makeup took five to six hours, The Shape of Water three. Creature Actor Doug Jones Shares How He Transforms Into Movie Monsters: Only its title was read, as the page didn’t load. It names 'creature actor' as a role, and transformation into monsters. (two of its three sources read directly and the remaining one from a search summary, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: The sources center on one performer (Doug Jones) whose films (Pan’s Labyrinth, The Shape of Water) are fantasy as much as creature features. No source states the suit performer as a convention of the creature feature as a whole. The claim about reacting to nothing was general visual-effects practice, and isn’t used here.
    How its scenes are played. The creature performer acts in suits with restricted senses; the others play terror of a thing that isn’t on set. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    States At The Limit: Fear, grief or exhaustion at their extreme, sustained, and believed though the threat is often not in the room. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and execution, and calls on your emotional depth and physical expression. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and interpretation, and calls on your imagination. The breakdown: the world’s rules have to be worked out before they can be believed.

## Cyberpunk (cyberpunk)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A subgenre of science fiction set in a bleak, high-tech near future where advanced computing and cybernetics coexist with social decay and corporate oppression.
    What it asks of an actor. Cyberpunk is defined by setting and theme (dystopian high-tech cities, corporate power, outsider protagonists, noir influence), not by fight work. The Matrix’s cast trained for months with Yuen Woo-ping because the directors expected them to do many of their own stunts; that is one film.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Fights And Stunts) belongs to action-led cyberpunk in the mold of The Matrix, with its martial-arts and wirework set pieces, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: The Matrix: how the Wachowskis changed sci-fi: "The Wachowskis expected their cast to do many of their own stunts, and so Reeves and co spent months training with legendary Hong Kong martial arts choreographer and director Yuen Woo-ping." Its account of the film’s influence is about cinematography, costume and comic-book films, not about cyberpunk acting. What is Cyberpunk? Genre Definition, Origins & Examples: It defines cyberpunk as "a science-fiction sub-genre dealing with the integration of society and technology in dystopian settings" and lists setting, technology, outsider protagonists, noir influence and transhumanist themes. It mentions no fights, martial arts or acting demands. It’s an industry education site, used only for the definition. (both its sources read directly, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: No credible source was found that says post-Matrix cyberpunk films as a group adopted wire-fu; the claims seen to that effect were on fan or listicle sites. The SCMP page from the earlier research couldn’t be opened, and wasn’t read again.
    How its scenes are played. A high-tech city and its corporations, made concrete, with the noir threat under it. The research found nothing on how it’s played, so this is estimated from its conventions.
    Belief In What Isn’t There: An invented world made concrete: the creature, the machine or the magic played as specifically as a cup of tea. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and interpretation, and calls on your imagination. The breakdown: the world’s rules have to be worked out before they can be believed.
    Suspense Held: A threat kept alive under every line: what’s at stake never dropped, even in the quiet. ## Dance (dance)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A film, also called screen dance, in which dancing is used to reveal the film’s challenges and central themes, whether narrative or formal.
    What it asks of an actor. Screendance asks its performers for trained dance: choreographed movement made specifically to be filmed, shaped with the camera and the edit.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Dance (yours: 51.7 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: The conservatory glossary defines the genre as combining dance and cinematography, with the dancers’ performances as its subject; BFI names trained dancers (Talley Beatty, Tudor’s ballet dancers) as its performers.
    What the research says: SFCD: 'Screendance, also known as dance film or dance on screen, is a genre of filmmaking that combines dance and cinematography'; the dancers’ movement is 'captured and presented on screen' with multiple angles, close-ups and editing that 'highlight the nuances of the choreography and the dancers' performances.' BFI: in A Study in Choreography for Camera, 'Dancer Talley Beatty moves freely ... with careful choreographed movements'; Deren 'collaborated with ... Antony Tudor, who brought a young crew of ballet dancers.' (read directly; high confidence).
    Still uncertain: Neither source speaks to stamina or to repeating choreography across takes; that part of the earlier reading is unsourced. A search summary (edukatesg.com, not used) says dancers need screen-specific timing before the shoot day.
    How its scenes are played. Dance reveals the story, so feeling is carried outward, in the body, at full size. The research found nothing on how it’s played, so this is estimated from its conventions.
    Played Large: Feeling worn on the outside and sized to fill the frame: the voice and the body carry it as much as the face. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and connection, and calls on your vocal expression, physical expression and presence. Rehearsing on your feet: a size the body and the voice can carry has to be found aloud and standing.
