## Dark Fantasy (dark_fantasy)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A subgenre of fantasy that combines magical and supernatural elements with the grim, unsettling and macabre atmosphere of horror.
    What it asks of an actor. Dark-fantasy creature roles can ask an actor for hours of prosthetic makeup and a physically draining performance through it, sometimes in a language the actor doesn’t speak.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Physical Transformation) belongs to creature roles in prosthetics, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Jones: the Faun and Pale Man makeups 'were both about five hours'; 'I couldn’t sit in a conventional chair... with the ram horns on the head, to hold my head up was like ugh, I can’t take it anymore.' He learned his Spanish lines phonetically. Wikipedia’s 'Dark fantasy' and 'Creature actor' pages (used only as leads) define the genre as fantasy with horror’s grim elements and a creature actor as one who plays a being of radically different appearance using suits or prosthetics. (read directly; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: This is one actor in one film, and a creature-performer specialism; the source doesn’t say that every lead in dark fantasy does prosthetic work. 'Morally ambiguous characterisation' from the earlier reading is unsourced. On review (October 10, 2026), one actor in one creature role wasn’t enough to make it a requirement of the whole genre.
    How its scenes are played. Creature roles in heavy prosthetics, physically draining, inside a grim invented world. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Belief In What Isn’t There: An invented world made concrete: the creature, the machine or the magic played as specifically as a cup of tea. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and interpretation, and calls on your imagination. The breakdown: the world’s rules have to be worked out before they can be believed.
    States At The Limit: Fear, grief or exhaustion at their extreme, sustained, and believed though the threat is often not in the room. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and execution, and calls on your emotional depth and physical expression. The lines, then rehearsal on your feet: an extreme state has to be found in the body, on lines you no longer think about.

## Detective (detective)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A story dealing with the step-by-step investigation and solution of a crime, usually murder, by a professional or amateur detective.
    What it asks of an actor. Long-running detective roles have asked actors for exhaustive character research, and in some versions for an accent and for rapid, exact delivery of deduction speeches.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: Suchet: he 'plowed through most of Agatha Christie’s novels' compiling a file of Poirot’s characteristics; 'I was desperate that he should sound French'; Poirot’s mannerisms 'have to be real and not jokey.' Search summaries, secondhand, say Cumberbatch found the deduction scenes hard because they 'have to be spot on' and involved a large volume of lines. (read directly; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: Both sources are about single famous characters, not the genre. Poirot’s accent belongs to the character, not to detective fiction, so the research didn’t name Accents. No source was found that says detective drama as a form asks a particular skill; the claim about expository revelation scenes rests on a search summary about one series.
    How its scenes are played. Long-running detectives asked for rapid, exact delivery of deduction speeches. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Dense Text, Exact: A lot of words, or difficult ones, delivered fluently and at speed: jargon, argument or deduction as second nature. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and interpretation, and calls on your memory and focus. Learning the lines: fluency at speed is the lines learned past knowing them.
    Suspense Held: A threat kept alive under every line: what’s at stake never dropped, even in the quiet. A self-tape in it is judged more on consistency and interpretation, and calls on your focus and listening and responsiveness. The breakdown, then rehearsal: suspense is held by knowing at every moment what your character fears will happen, and keeping it alive through the scene.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

Source for what it asks: Strand Magazine, 'Interview: David Suchet' — https://strandmag.com/the-magazine/interviews/david-suchet/; search summaries on Benedict Cumberbatch's Sherlock (Collider interview, https://collider.com/benedict-cumberbatch-interview-sherlock-war-horse-spielberg/, not read directly).
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.

## Disaster (disaster)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A film genre that has an impending or ongoing disaster, natural or man-made, as its subject and primary plot device.
    What it asks of an actor. Disaster films can put actors through weeks of physically punishing practical work, such as water-tank sequences done in person rather than by doubles.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Fights And Stunts) belongs to productions that shoot their peril practically, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Watts: 'I had to stay in a water tank for weeks. (So) it was quite physically demanding for me'; 'we were anchored into a chair with weights on us ... the chair starts spinning, and you have to do all your arm-acting and head-flipping'; underwater work meant holding her breath. Search summaries of NPR (not read directly) and Wikipedia add 'probably the most physically demanding thing I’ve ever done' and five weeks in the tank with Tom Holland. (read directly; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: It’s one film. A search summary of a blog, secondhand, reports Ernest Borgnine calling The Poseidon Adventure his most physically demanding film; that isn’t verified. Nothing was found on large-ensemble panic work as a convention. On review (October 10, 2026), one film’s water-tank work wasn’t enough to make it a requirement of the whole genre.
    How its scenes are played. Actors go through weeks of physically punishing practical work in person. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    States At The Limit: Fear, grief or exhaustion at their extreme, sustained, and believed though the threat is often not in the room. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and execution, and calls on your emotional depth and physical expression. The lines, then rehearsal on your feet: an extreme state has to be found in the body, on lines you no longer think about.

## Docudrama (docudrama)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A drama, as for television, dealing freely with historical events, especially of a recent and controversial nature.
    What it asks of an actor. Docudrama asks actors to stand in for real, often recent or living, people, and the audience judges the performance against what it already knows of them.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Character Work (yours: 52.7 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: Bignell’s abstract places recognition of the real person at the center of docudrama performance; the Backstage summary has actors noting the voice and mannerisms of the people they’ll play. This is character work led by impersonation, not prosthetics.
    What the research says: Bignell (only the abstract, through a search, as the PDF couldn’t be opened): docudrama’s performers invite viewers to judge them in relation to real events and other media representations, evaluating 'the performance of the real' against prior knowledge. Britannica Dictionary: 'a movie that is usually made for television and that tells a story about real events that have happened recently.' A search summary of Backstage ('The Actor as Interviewer', not read directly) says actors in a docudrama about the People’s Temple interviewed survivors and noted clothing, voice and mannerisms. (only the abstract read; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: The scholarly chapter was read only as an abstract. Whether Physical Transformation, which the game trains as Character Work, is the right skill for impersonation is a judgment call; no source mentions prosthetics or makeup for docudrama.
    How its scenes are played. Recent events dramatized freely, with a documentary’s immediacy, by actors who must become the real people. The research found nothing on how it’s played, so this is estimated from its conventions.
    Caught Live: Behavior that looks unrehearsed: real listening, the next line taken from what the other person just did. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your listening and responsiveness and adaptability. Rehearsing on your feet: what looks unrehearsed is found by playing it through until nothing is set but the wants.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/docudrama (Merriam-Webster). Source for what it asks: Jonathan Bignell, 'Docudrama performance: realism, recognition and representation', in Christine Cornea (ed.), Genre and Performance: Film and Television (Manchester University Press, 2010), pp. 59-75 — https://centaur.reading.ac.uk/17671/1/Docudrama_Performance.pdf; Britannica Dictionary, 'docudrama' — https://www.britannica.com/dictionary/docudrama.
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.
  ways: Character Work (What it asks for: Physical Transformation) | Listening And Responsiveness (Its scenes call on it: Caught Live) | Adaptability (Its scenes call on it: Caught Live)

## Docufiction (docufiction)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.

    Its conventions. The cinematographic combination of documentary and fiction, in which real people and settings are placed within a fictionalized narrative.
    What it asks of an actor. Docufiction’s main characters are often non-professionals essentially playing themselves, unscripted, with improvised dialogue and genuine reactions.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Improvisation (yours: 52.0 of 90) , On Camera (yours: 51.9 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: The page says docufictions are usually unscripted, with improvisational dialogue. Participants portray 'their own genuine reactions' on camera, which is naturalistic on-camera behavior as a convention.
    What the research says: 'The main character or characters—often portrayed by non-professional or amateur actors—are essentially playing themselves'; docufictions 'are usually not scripted' and use participants’ 'own genuine reactions and their own improvisational dialogue'. (read directly; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: The participants are often not actors at all, so what it asks of an actor applies loosely.
    How its scenes are played. Its main characters are often non-professionals playing themselves, unscripted, with genuine reactions. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Caught Live: Behavior that looks unrehearsed: real listening, the next line taken from what the other person just did. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your listening and responsiveness and adaptability. Rehearsing on your feet: what looks unrehearsed is found by playing it through until nothing is set but the wants.

## Documentary (documentary)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A motion picture that shapes and interprets factual material for purposes of education or entertainment.
    What it asks of an actor. Actors in documentaries mostly appear as voices reading letters or narration, or as performers in reenactments, sometimes lip-syncing to real interview audio.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Voice-Over (yours: 54.0 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: Yates describes actors voicing the quoted words of real people in documentary voice-overs, and that their tone shapes the meaning.
    What the research says: Yates: reenactments 'provide a context, to show what things looked or sounded like'; on voice-overs, 'the tone of voice used by the actor is likely to give the audience the impression that the individual quoted was, indeed, given to the emotion associated with that tone.' Shambu: in The Arbor 'actors lip-sync to audio recordings of Dunbar’s family members, following the practices of "verbatim theater"'; in Framing Agnes the performers’ lines 'are drawn from the original interview transcripts.' (read directly; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: Neither source says how common actor narration is relative to narration by the filmmaker or a presenter. 'Little conventional acting' is a reasonable reading of the form but no source states it.
    How its scenes are played. Actors in documentaries read letters and narration: plain and meant, never performed at the audience. The research found nothing on how it’s played, so this is estimated from its conventions.
    Played Straight: Meant completely: the feeling played with full conviction, never winked at, however large or strange the material. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and connection, and calls on your emotional depth and presence. The breakdown and the lines: believing it starts with knowing exactly what your character wants and why it matters to them.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.

## Domestic Drama (domestic_drama)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Drama centered on the private and family affairs of ordinary, usually middle-class, people, not on public matters of state (descended from the older form called domestic tragedy).
    What it asks of an actor. Domestic drama descends from the realist and naturalist stage, which asked for psychologically real acting and for ensemble playing in place of the star turn.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: Britannica (from a search summary): Stanislavsky 'began with attempts to find a style of acting more appropriate to the greater realism of 20th-century drama than the histrionic acting styles of the 19th century.' Fiveable (a study guide, from a search summary): the Meiningen Players 'pioneered the concept of ensemble acting'; realist theater 'emphasized ensemble acting, with all members of the cast working together to create a cohesive, believable world.' (from a search summary; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: The sources concern realist and naturalist drama (Ibsen, Chekhov, Strindberg), the tradition domestic drama belongs to, not 'domestic drama' by name. They describe stage acting, so they don’t support Naturalism On Camera. Britannica couldn’t be opened, so it wasn’t read directly.
    How its scenes are played. Descended from the realist stage, which asked for psychologically real acting and ensemble playing. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Played Between Two: The scene lives between the two of you: chemistry, contrast, a part each that only works against the other. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your presence, listening and responsiveness and emotional depth. Rehearsing on your feet: a partnership is built in the exchange, played through until each part answers the other.
    Played Straight: Meant completely: the feeling played with full conviction, never winked at, however large or strange the material. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and connection, and calls on your emotional depth and presence. The breakdown and the lines: believing it starts with knowing exactly what your character wants and why it matters to them.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll read Hedda Gabler, by Henrik Ibsen, a play of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://www.oxfordreference.com/display/10.1093/oi/authority.20110803095725766 (Oxford Reference, 'domestic tragedy'; Wikipedia’s 'Domestic drama' was found too). Source for what it asks: From search summaries: Britannica, 'Stanislavsky system' — https://www.britannica.com/art/Stanislavsky-system (couldn’t be opened); fiveable, 'Realist and Naturalist theater' — https://fiveable.me/18th-and-19th-century-literature/unit-6/realist-naturalist-theater/study-guide/IoXCgOsbj4UmitM2.
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.
  ways: Presence (Its scenes call on it: Played Between Two, Played Straight) | Listening And Responsiveness (Its scenes call on it: Played Between Two) | Emotional Depth (Its scenes call on it: Played Between Two, Played Straight)
  provenance: Its definition was taken from a search summary, and what it asks is held with medium confidence.

## Drama (drama)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A play, film or television program about a serious subject that isn’t primarily meant to make the audience laugh.
    What it asks of an actor. Serious drama in the modern tradition asks for realistic, emotionally truthful acting, the aim of Stanislavsky’s system and the training built on it.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: From search summaries, as the pages couldn’t be opened: the system was an attempt to find acting 'more appropriate to the greater realism of 20th-century drama'; it 'emphasizes actors drawing on emotional memory and intense inner identification with their characters.' (from a search summary; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: Britannica couldn’t be opened, so the wording is from search summaries. 'Drama' is too broad for any one of the requirements the game can ask for, and emotional truth isn’t one of them. Nothing ties Naturalism On Camera to drama as such.
    How its scenes are played. Serious drama asks for realistic, emotionally truthful acting, the aim of Stanislavsky’s system. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Played Straight: Meant completely: the feeling played with full conviction, never winked at, however large or strange the material. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and connection, and calls on your emotional depth and presence. The breakdown and the lines: believing it starts with knowing exactly what your character wants and why it matters to them.

## Dystopian (dystopian)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Fiction set in a dystopia, an imagined society in which people are unhappy and usually afraid because they aren’t treated fairly; the opposite of a utopia.
    What it asks of an actor. No source was found for what dystopian fiction as a genre asks of actors. One production, Children of Men, asked for rehearsed choreography of long takes.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: From search summaries: Cuarón shot as many long takes as possible; 'the long takes required a higher level of choreography from cast and crew' and more rehearsal time. Search summaries of Collider praise restrained performances in The Hunger Games films, which is a critic’s opinion, not a statement of what the genre asks. (read secondhand; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: Nothing found supports 'suppressed fear and restrained, controlled performance' as a genre demand. The long-take choreography belongs to one director’s method, not to dystopian fiction.
    How its scenes are played. An imagined society people are afraid in: its rules made concrete, and fear kept off the face. The research found nothing on how it’s played, so this is estimated from its conventions.
    Belief In What Isn’t There: An invented world made concrete: the creature, the machine or the magic played as specifically as a cup of tea. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and interpretation, and calls on your imagination. The breakdown: the world’s rules have to be worked out before they can be believed.
    Feeling Held In: The feeling is there and kept under: stillness, economy, what isn’t said carrying what is. A self-tape in it is judged more on consistency and believability, and calls on your composure and focus. Learning the lines and rehearsing on your feet: control comes from knowing it well enough to hold it still.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.

## Eco-Horror (eco_horror)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A subgenre of horror, also called eco-terror, concerned with human fear for and of the non-human natural world, often as a consequence of human damage to it.
    What it asks of an actor. No source was found for what eco-horror asks of actors. One production report says the Annihilation cast weren’t told when a creature would attack, to get real reactions.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: From a search summary: 'the cast wasn’t aware when a monster would come out and attack them during a particular sequence, adding to the authentic reactions captured on screen.' (from a search summary; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: It’s one film, unverified. Calling Annihilation eco-horror is the search result’s placement, not a scholar’s. 'Survival-driven physical performance' and reacting to effects-generated threats as a genre demand are unsourced.
    How its scenes are played. One production kept its cast from knowing when the creature would attack, for real reactions. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    States At The Limit: Fear, grief or exhaustion at their extreme, sustained, and believed though the threat is often not in the room. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and execution, and calls on your emotional depth and physical expression. The lines, then rehearsal on your feet: an extreme state has to be found in the body, on lines you no longer think about.
    Caught Live: Behavior that looks unrehearsed: real listening, the next line taken from what the other person just did. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your listening and responsiveness and adaptability. Rehearsing on your feet: what looks unrehearsed is found by playing it through until nothing is set but the wants.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

## Epic (epic)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A film genre of grand scale and sweeping scope, typically dealing with historical events or legendary heroic figures, with large casts and lavish settings.
    What it asks of an actor. The epic is defined by scale, spectacle and large casts, not by an acting style. Physical prowess is tied to the hero in some strands (the Fairbanks swashbucklers, the sword-and-sandal peplum), and battle-heavy productions train their leads in combat, but no credible source makes combat a convention of the whole genre.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Fights And Stunts) belongs to battle and sword-and-sandal epics, and their warrior and swashbuckler leads, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Epic Films: It focuses on scale, spectacle, history and technology. On performers only: in Fairbanks’s 1920s epics 'the power of the central character to effect change is, however fancifully, bound up with his physical prowess'. It says nothing on battles, stunts or training as a requirement of the genre. Breaking the Physique Barrier: Steve Reeves and the Promotion of Hercules: After Hercules (1958), sword-and-sandal epics starred 'weight-trained actors'. This supports physique for the peplum subgenre, not combat for epics generally. The page couldn’t be opened. Russell Crowe interview, Gladiator 20th anniversary: Not read, as it’s behind a paywall. Earlier search summaries say Crowe trained for fighting and was injured; that’s one film only. (one of its three sources read directly and the other two from search summaries, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: The evidence for combat is anecdotal, production by production; the Variety interview and the peplum paper weren’t read. Many epics (biblical, biographical, romantic epics) have no combat lead.
    How its scenes are played. Scale and spectacle, with the hero’s physical prowess in its strands. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Command Of The Frame: Status and threat carried in the body and the look more than the words: the person everyone in the room watches. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.
    Played Large: Feeling worn on the outside and sized to fill the frame: the voice and the body carry it as much as the face. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and connection, and calls on your vocal expression, physical expression and presence. Rehearsing on your feet: a size the body and the voice can carry has to be found aloud and standing.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epic_film (Wikipedia; filmsite.org’s page on the AFI’s genres was found too). Source for what it asks: Steve Neale, Epic Films (Schirmer Encyclopedia of Film, via Encyclopedia.com, 2007), https://www.encyclopedia.com/arts/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/epic-films [read directly]; David Chapman (attribution from search listing, not verified), Breaking the Physique Barrier: Steve Reeves and the Promotion of Hercules (ResearchGate / LA84 digital library, unknown), https://www.researchgate.net/publication/277775916_Breaking_the_Physique_Barrier_Steve_Reeves_and_the_Promotion_of_Hercules [from a search summary]; Variety (byline not verified), Russell Crowe interview, Gladiator 20th anniversary (Variety, 2020), https://variety.com/2020/film/news/russell-crowe-interview-gladiator-20th-anniversary-1234596980/ [from a search summary].
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.
  ways: Presence (Its scenes call on it: Command Of The Frame, Played Large) | Physical Expression (Its scenes call on it: Command Of The Frame, Played Large) | Vocal Expression (Its scenes call on it: Played Large)
  provenance: Its definition was taken from a search summary, and what it asks is held with low confidence.

## Erotic Thriller (erotic_thriller)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A film subgenre of the thriller with a thematic basis in illicit romance or sexual fantasy, holding pleasure and danger in close proximity.
    What it asks of an actor. Erotic thrillers put choreographed intimate scenes at the center of the form, and productions now stage them with intimacy coordinators.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Intimacy (yours: 53.3 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: The source names the erotic thriller as a form whose sex scenes are choreographed by an intimacy specialist; the genre’s own definition (illicit romance or sexual fantasy) calls for such scenes.
    What the research says: From search summaries: 'The sex scenes in the 1996 erotic crime thriller Bound were choreographed by ... Susie Bright, two decades before the "intimacy coordinator" title existed'; 'When doing a film like an erotic thriller or a psychosexual thriller, coordinators need to ensure the well being of the talent.' (from a search summary; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: The article couldn’t be read past the paywall. Nothing found on the 'guarded, deceptive' playing the earlier reading claimed.
    How its scenes are played. Pleasure and danger held close, with choreographed intimate scenes at the center. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Suspense Held: A threat kept alive under every line: what’s at stake never dropped, even in the quiet. A self-tape in it is judged more on consistency and interpretation, and calls on your focus and listening and responsiveness. The breakdown, then rehearsal: suspense is held by knowing at every moment what your character fears will happen, and keeping it alive through the scene.
    Played Between Two: The scene lives between the two of you: chemistry, contrast, a part each that only works against the other. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your presence, listening and responsiveness and emotional depth. Rehearsing on your feet: a partnership is built in the exchange, played through until each part answers the other.

    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.

## Espionage (espionage)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.

    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read

    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A genre, also called the spy film or spy thriller, that deals with fictional espionage, either realistically or as a basis for fantasy.
    What it asks of an actor. The realist strand of the spy film has asked for extreme restraint and stillness, characters who keep everything in; the genre also runs to fantasy (Bond), so no single demand holds across it.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: From search summaries: Oldman, 'It’s interesting to be the motor of a scene, but from a very restrained, almost passive place'; he moved from 'dangerously emotional people' to 'people who have to keep everything so restrained and unemotional.' An NPR piece on The Spy (from a search summary) says Sacha Baron Cohen saw the real agent as sustaining a false identity like an actor. (from a search summary; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: This is one film’s approach. Naturalism On Camera was considered and left out: the restraint is described for le Carré adaptations only, and the genre’s own definition covers fantasy spy films too. Accents, disguise and action work are unsourced as things the genre asks.
    How its scenes are played. Its realist strand asked for extreme restraint and stillness; its fantasy strand for the star who owns the room. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Feeling Held In: The feeling is there and kept under: stillness, economy, what isn’t said carrying what is. A self-tape in it is judged more on consistency and believability, and calls on your composure and focus. Learning the lines and rehearsing on your feet: control comes from knowing it well enough to hold it still.
    Command Of The Frame: Status and threat carried in the body and the look more than the words: the person everyone in the room watches. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

## Experimental (experimental)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A mode of filmmaking, also called avant-garde cinema, that doesn’t apply standard cinematic conventions, adopting non-narrative forms or alternatives to traditional narratives and methods.
    What it asks of an actor. One canonical strand of experimental film, Maya Deren’s 'choreocinema', fused dance and film with trained dancers. Reference sources describe experimental film overall as non-narrative and convention-resisting, with no performance norm.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Dancing) belongs to the dance-film strand of experimental film (choreocinema, or screendance), not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Maya Deren: 7 films that guarantee her legend: Dance critic John Martin coined 'choreocinema' for 'the way Deren linked dance and film'; Deren worked with dancers Talley Beatty and Chao-Li Chi. It presents this as Deren’s distinctive contribution, not a general feature of experimental film. Experimental and Avant-Garde Film: Experimental film is 'principally non-narrative' and better understood as 'a type of method' than a genre; its only performance example is Warhol’s Sleep (poet John Giorno asleep), not dance. (both its sources read directly, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: Experimental film is defined by refusing conventions. Its documented performers include sleepers and non-actors as readily as dancers.
    How its scenes are played. Non-narrative forms; one canonical strand fused trained dance and film. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    The World’s Own Logic: A world with rules of its own, absurd, dreamlike or satirical, played wholeheartedly by them and never commented on. A self-tape in it is judged more on interpretation and execution, and calls on your imagination and physical expression. The breakdown: the world’s logic has to be found before it can be played as if it were sense.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Experimental_film (Wikipedia; EBSCO Research Starters was found too). Source for what it asks: Georgia Korossi, Maya Deren: 7 films that guarantee her legend (BFI, 2017), https://www.bfi.org.uk/features/maya-deren-meshes-of-the-afternoon [read directly]; Bill Kte'pi, Experimental and Avant-Garde Film (EBSCO Research Starters, 2021), https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/film/experimental-and-avant-garde-film/ [read directly].
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.
  ways: Imagination (Its scenes call on it: The World’s Own Logic) | Physical Expression (Its scenes call on it: The World’s Own Logic)
  provenance: Its definition was taken from a search summary, and what it asks is held with low confidence.

## Exploitation (exploitation)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A film that seeks commercial success by capitalizing on current trends, niche genres or sensational content such as sex, violence and the bizarre, usually on a low budget.
    What it asks of an actor. Exploitation films were shot in days on tiny budgets with unknown actors, and their selling point was nudity, sex and gore, which the performers supplied.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Intimacy (yours: 53.3 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: Schaefer describes nudity, striptease and sexploitation as the form’s core spectacle, performed by its cast.
    What the research says: 'Their low budgets and accelerated shooting schedules meant that exploitation films featured stilted performances'; 'Shooting schedules were less than a week, with some films being shot in as little as two or three days'; scenes of spectacle included 'scenes in a nudist camp' and 'women performing striptease dances'; 'nudie-cuties'; sexploitation films; Blood Feast’s disemboweling. (read directly; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: Nothing in the source says exploitation performers did their own stunts, so the research didn’t name Fights And Stunts. 'Broad, uninhibited performance' is unsourced; Schaefer calls the performances 'stilted', which is a result of the conditions, not something the genre asks.
    How its scenes are played. Shot in days, sold on sensation that its performers supplied at full size. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Played Large: Feeling worn on the outside and sized to fill the frame: the voice and the body carry it as much as the face. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and connection, and calls on your vocal expression, physical expression and presence. Rehearsing on your feet: a size the body and the voice can carry has to be found aloud and standing.
    States At The Limit: Fear, grief or exhaustion at their extreme, sustained, and believed though the threat is often not in the room. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and execution, and calls on your emotional depth and physical expression. The lines, then rehearsal on your feet: an extreme state has to be found in the body, on lines you no longer think about.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exploitation_film (Wikipedia; Dartmouth College’s film-genre research guide was found too). Source for what it asks: Eric Schaefer, 'Exploitation Films', Schirmer Encyclopedia of Film, via Encyclopedia.com — https://www.encyclopedia.com/arts/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/exploitation-films.
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.
  ways: Intimacy (What it asks for: Staged Intimacy) | Vocal Expression (Its scenes call on it: Played Large) | Physical Expression (Its scenes call on it: Played Large, States At The Limit) | Presence (Its scenes call on it: Played Large) | Emotional Depth (Its scenes call on it: States At The Limit)
  provenance: Its definition was taken from a search summary, and what it asks is held with medium confidence.

    Its conventions. A wonder tale involving marvelous elements and occurrences, though not necessarily about fairies, embracing both popular folktales and later invented art tales.
    What it asks of an actor. No source was found for what fairy-tale film asks of actors.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: Pantomime sources, from search summaries, describe fairy tales as its source material and physical comedy and stock characters as its style; this is about British pantomime, not fairy-tale film. (read secondhand; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: Archetypal, heightened characterization and prosthetics are unsourced. What pantomime asks wasn’t carried over to fairy tale, because that would stretch a source about another form.
    How its scenes are played. A wonder tale: marvels made real, told with the clear shapes of the folktale. The research found nothing on how it’s played, so this is estimated from its conventions.

A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and interpretation, and calls on your imagination. The breakdown: the world’s rules have to be worked out before they can be believed.

A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and connection, and calls on your vocal expression, physical expression and presence.     Its conventions. Imaginative fiction that relies on strangeness of setting, such as other worlds or times, and of characters, such as supernatural or unnatural beings.
    What it asks of an actor. Major fantasy productions have taught their casts invented languages and purpose-built accents, and asked some actors for heavy physical transformation such as stilts and motion capture.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Accents, Physical Transformation) belongs to particular productions with invented languages, and creature or motion-capture roles, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.

(read directly; low confidence).

On review (October 10, 2026), two productions’ invented languages and transformations weren’t enough to make them requirements of the whole genre.

    Its conventions. A form of comedy that relies on highly implausible plots, stereotyped or exaggerated characters, physical humor and misunderstandings.
    What it asks of an actor. Farce calls for highly stylized performances and dramatic reactions, with adept comic timing for wordplay, visual gags and slapstick.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Comedy (yours: 57.7 of 90) , Clown And Mask (yours: 57.6 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.

    What the research says: 'broad, larger-than-life characters, often calling for highly stylized performances and dramatic reactions'; 'There may be wordplay, visual gags and slapstick, requiring the need for adept comic timing by the actors.' (read directly; medium confidence).

## Film Noir (film_noir)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A film genre offering dark or fatalistic interpretations of reality, applied to US crime films of the 1940s and 1950s noted for cynical characters and stark, expressionistic lighting.
    What it asks of an actor. Film noir’s performers carried the hard-boiled school’s terse, cynical dialogue, and the genre leaned on first-person voice-over narration spoken by its protagonists.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Voice-Over (yours: 54.0 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: Schrader lists voice-over narration as characteristic across canonical noirs; the narration is the actor’s voice-only performance.
    What the research says: From search summaries: the hard-boiled writers 'created the "tough", cynical way of acting and thinking'; 'a love of romantic narration' in The Postman Always Rings Twice, Laura, Double Indemnity, The Lady from Shanghai, Out of the Past and Sunset Boulevard, where narration creates 'a mood of temps perdu'. A search summary of No Film School adds 'terse, understated vernacular peppered with laconic wisecracks'. (read secondhand; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: Schrader’s essay was seen only through reproductions and search summaries, not the original. Britannica’s film-noir entry couldn’t be opened. 'Cool, clipped delivery' as a performance demand is inferred from the dialogue style, not stated about actors.
    How its scenes are played. Its performers carried terse, cynical, hard-boiled dialogue and voice-over. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Command Of The Frame: Status and threat carried in the body and the look more than the words: the person everyone in the room watches. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.
    Feeling Held In: The feeling is there and kept under: stillness, economy, what isn’t said carrying what is. A self-tape in it is judged more on consistency and believability, and calls on your composure and focus. Learning the lines and rehearsing on your feet: control comes from knowing it well enough to hold it still.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

## Folk Horror (folk_horror)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A subgenre of horror that uses elements of folklore, typically rural settings, isolation, superstition, paganism and sacrifice, to invoke fear and foreboding.
    What it asks of an actor. Folk horror is defined by landscape, isolation, a community’s skewed beliefs and a climactic ritual. In The Wicker Man the islanders perform folk songs and dance on screen, but that is one film, its lead’s singing wasn’t her own, and a scholar of the film calls horror films whose characters perform their own music exceptionally uncommon.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Singing) belongs to folk horror that stages its community ritual as performed folk song (The Wicker Man and films modeled on it), not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Introduction: Defining Folk Horror: Folk horror rests on invented folklore, deep landscape and a 'monstrous tribe' bound by belief; "The rituals that often conclude folk horror narratives ... bond the communities"; and it stresses "embodied traditions and rituals". It doesn’t name song or dance as conventions. The Diegetic Folk Horrors of The Wicker Man (1973): It describes bar patrons playing and singing 'The Landlord’s Daughter' and Willow joining a dance, and notes that horror films whose characters perform their own music are exceptionally uncommon. The Wicker Man: Britt Ekland Goes Behind the Making of a Folk Horror Classic: Ekland’s dialogue was dubbed by Annie Ross, and a body double was used for parts of the nude dance. (Search summaries add that Rachel Verney sang 'Willow’s Song'; that wasn’t read at the source.) (all three of its sources read directly, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: Scovell’s 'folk horror chain' (Folk Horror: Hours Dreadful and Things Strange, Auteur, 2017) was seen only through secondary summaries and names no song or dance. Recent films such as Midsommar stage ritual dance, but no credible source was found that generalizes it.
    How its scenes are played. A community’s skewed beliefs and its ritual, played by their own logic, and the dread of the outsider. The research found nothing on how it’s played, so this is estimated from its conventions.
    Suspense Held: A threat kept alive under every line: what’s at stake never dropped, even in the quiet. A self-tape in it is judged more on consistency and interpretation, and calls on your focus and listening and responsiveness. The breakdown, then rehearsal: suspense is held by knowing at every moment what your character fears will happen, and keeping it alive through the scene.
    The World’s Own Logic: A world with rules of its own, absurd, dreamlike or satirical, played wholeheartedly by them and never commented on. A self-tape in it is judged more on interpretation and execution, and calls on your imagination and physical expression. The breakdown: the world’s logic has to be found before it can be played as if it were sense.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_horror (Wikipedia; the BFI’s coverage was found too). Source for what it asks: Dawn Keetley, Introduction: Defining Folk Horror (Revenant: Critical and Creative Studies of the Supernatural, issue 5 (Folk Horror), 2020), https://www.revenantjournal.com/contents/introduction-defining-folk-horror-2/ [read directly]; Jesse Pasternack, The Diegetic Folk Horrors of The Wicker Man (1973) (Establishing Shot (Indiana University blogs), 2023), https://blogs.iu.edu/establishingshot/2023/09/25/the-diegetic-folk-horrors-of-the-wicker-man-1973/ [read directly]; Don Kaye, The Wicker Man: Britt Ekland Goes Behind the Making of a Folk Horror Classic (Den of Geek, 2023), https://www.denofgeek.com/movies/the-wicker-man-britt-ekland-building-pagan-icon-horror/ [read directly].

## Found Footage (found_footage)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.

    Its conventions. A cinematic technique and film genre in which all or most of the work is presented as recordings made by characters in the story and later found.
    What it asks of an actor. Found-footage films routinely use improvisation and naturalistic acting, and the actors may shoot the film themselves as they perform.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Improvisation (yours: 52.0 of 90) , On Camera (yours: 51.9 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: The page says improvisation is 'routinely employed'. Naturalistic acting is 'routinely employed', the page says, and actors may operate the camera.
    What the research says: 'the cinematography may be done by the actors themselves as they perform'; 'shaky camera work, improvisation and naturalistic acting are routinely employed'. (read directly; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: Wikipedia was used only as a lead.
    How its scenes are played. It routinely uses improvisation and naturalistic acting, in fear, often shot by the actors themselves. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Caught Live: Behavior that looks unrehearsed: real listening, the next line taken from what the other person just did. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your listening and responsiveness and adaptability. Rehearsing on your feet: what looks unrehearsed is found by playing it through until nothing is set but the wants.
    States At The Limit: Fear, grief or exhaustion at their extreme, sustained, and believed though the threat is often not in the room. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and execution, and calls on your emotional depth and physical expression. The lines, then rehearsal on your feet: an extreme state has to be found in the body, on lines you no longer think about.

    What it asks of an actor. The gangster film’s stars defined a tough, physically marked performance style, and sound made hard-boiled dialogue and gunfire central to the form.

## Giallo (giallo)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. An Italian crime film with murder-mystery elements, named for the yellow covers of Italian mystery paperbacks, typically featuring stylized murders, amateur sleuths and a black-gloved killer.
    What it asks of an actor. Gialli were shot without sync sound, often with an international cast each speaking their own language in the same take, and the whole soundtrack, dialogue included, was dubbed afterwards.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Voice-Led Performance) belongs to English-speaking leads who re-voiced themselves in post-synchronization, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: 'Italian films were almost exclusively shot without sync sound. Boom microphones were rarely used on set'; 'a British actor speaking English, an Italian actress speaking Italian, and a Spanish actor speaking Spanish—all in the very same take'; 'Voice actors were brought into recording booths to dub the dialogue into various languages.' A search summary of Backstage (not read directly) adds that English dubs often used the lead English-speaking actors’ own voices. (read directly; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: Voice-Led Performance is weak for the on-screen actor: it’s strongest for English-speaking leads who re-voiced themselves, a claim resting on a search summary of Backstage. 'Stylised, heightened terror' is unsourced. The CineMedia piece names no author. On review (October 10, 2026), that wasn’t enough to make it a requirement of the whole genre.
    How its scenes are played. Stylized murders and a mystery: suspense in a heightened, designed world. The research found nothing on how it’s played, so this is estimated from its conventions.
    Suspense Held: A threat kept alive under every line: what’s at stake never dropped, even in the quiet. A self-tape in it is judged more on consistency and interpretation, and calls on your focus and listening and responsiveness. The breakdown, then rehearsal: suspense is held by knowing at every moment what your character fears will happen, and keeping it alive through the scene.
    The World’s Own Logic: A world with rules of its own, absurd, dreamlike or satirical, played wholeheartedly by them and never commented on. A self-tape in it is judged more on interpretation and execution, and calls on your imagination and physical expression. The breakdown: the world’s logic has to be found before it can be played as if it were sense.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

Dubbing, Language, and the Affective Experience in Giallo Cinema' (no author shown) — https://cinemedia.media/in-what-tongue-do-gialli-speak/.

## Gothic Horror (gothic_horror)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Horror that draws on Gothic literature, with macabre narratives set in isolated, decaying places such as castles and old houses, supernatural events and tormented characters.
    What it asks of an actor. No credible source found describes a heightened or declamatory acting convention for gothic horror film. Ranger’s study of the Georgian Gothic stage records vocal and physical casting requirements (a bass voice for older tragic roles, a singing voice for the youthful hero), which concern the stage, not the film genre.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research holds this (Heightened Language) with low confidence, so the game doesn’t treat it as a requirement of the genre. The small print below says why.
    What the research says: Performing Arts and the Gothic (excerpting Paul Ranger, 'Terror and Pity Reign in Every Breast': Gothic Drama in the London Patent Theatres, 1750-1820, 1991): On the Gothic stage, 'a singing voice was a requisite for the part of the youthful hero' and older tragic roles needed 'a sturdy figure and a bass voice through which a range of disturbed passions could be expressed'. On reading the page, it doesn’t describe a declamatory style as such, and says nothing about film. Hammer and Beyond: The British Horror Film: Its title and author were verified as the standard scholarly study of Hammer gothic; no passage on acting style was found in what could be read. It’s cited only to record that the search reached scholarship and found nothing on performance. (one of its two sources read directly and the other from a search summary, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: The earlier claim overstated the Ranger excerpt. Hutchings’s book may discuss performance, but couldn’t be read. If anything, the theatrical style belongs to British horror drawn from stage melodrama (Tod Slaughter, Hammer), which is unsourced here.
    How its scenes are played. Gothic literature’s macabre, in decaying castles of another age. The research found nothing on how it’s played, so this is estimated from its conventions.

## Grindhouse (grindhouse)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Grindhouse names the low-budget horror, splatter, foreign and exploitation films for adults that played in American ‘grindhouse’ or ‘action house’ theaters, and films made in imitation of them.
    What it asks of an actor. Grindhouse-style films have prized performers who do their own stunts, for the gut reaction of visibly real action, alongside the speed and lurid content of the exploitation films they imitate.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Fights And Stunts) belongs to stunt performers in homage films, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Bell: doing her own stunts 'was like a stipulation! ... he said, "That’s part of the reason I’m casting you"'; an actor doing her own stunts gives 'a gut reaction, a sense of reality'. Schaefer: exploitation shoots were 'less than a week', with 'stilted performances' and gore spectacle. (read directly; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: Death Proof is a homage, and Bell is a stuntwoman by trade; nothing shows the original grindhouse circuit’s films used stunts the actors performed. 'Without retakes' is unsourced. On review (October 10, 2026), a homage and a stuntwoman by trade weren’t enough to make it a requirement of the whole genre.
    How its scenes are played. Performers who do their own stunts, for the gut reaction of visibly real action, at exploitation’s pitch. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Played Large: Feeling worn on the outside and sized to fill the frame: the voice and the body carry it as much as the face. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and connection, and calls on your vocal expression, physical expression and presence. Rehearsing on your feet: a size the body and the voice can carry has to be found aloud and standing.
    States At The Limit: Fear, grief or exhaustion at their extreme, sustained, and believed though the threat is often not in the room. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and execution, and calls on your emotional depth and physical expression. The lines, then rehearsal on your feet: an extreme state has to be found in the body, on lines you no longer think about.

## Hangout (hangout)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A hangout film prioritizes unhurried interaction among likeable characters over forward plot momentum, so that the pleasure lies in spending time in their company.
    What it asks of an actor. The hangout film, a term Tarantino coined for Rio Bravo, favors character and vibe over plot, so the audience spends time with characters who feel like friends. No credible source makes naturalistic acting a convention of the whole form; naturalism belongs to Linklater-style and mumblecore-adjacent films.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Naturalism On Camera) belongs to Linklater-style and mumblecore-adjacent naturalistic hangout films, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Howard Hawks, Rio Bravo, and the Hangout Film: The hangout film prioritizes 'vibe and character over pure narrative momentum'; Rio Bravo 'is the film in reference to which Quentin Tarantino coined the term'. It says nothing about naturalistic acting; its exemplar is a 1959 studio western. A Generation Finds Its Mumble: Mumblecore deals with nonprofessional actors and quasi-improvised dialogue; narratives 'hinge less on plot points than on the tipping points in interpersonal relationships'. It’s about mumblecore, a related but distinct form. Dazed and Confused: how Richard Linklater created the ultimate high-school experience: The page couldn’t be opened again. An earlier search summary: Linklater’s emphasis on naturalistic dialogue and cast chemistry. That’s one director. (one of its three sources read directly and the other two from search summaries, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: The hangout film is a critic’s category, not an established genre with a scholarly literature. Chemistry is emphasized in listicles, but none of the requirements the game can ask for covers it.
    How its scenes are played. Unhurried time with likeable characters: the pleasure is their interaction, not the plot. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Played Between Two: The scene lives between the two of you: chemistry, contrast, a part each that only works against the other. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your presence, listening and responsiveness and emotional depth. Rehearsing on your feet: a partnership is built in the exchange, played through until each part answers the other.
    Caught Live: Behavior that looks unrehearsed: real listening, the next line taken from what the other person just did. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your listening and responsiveness and adaptability. Rehearsing on your feet: what looks unrehearsed is found by playing it through until nothing is set but the wants.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://crookedmarquee.com/on-the-singular-pleasures-of-the-hangout-movie/ (a film criticism site; Quentin Tarantino popularized the term). Source for what it asks: Chris Forrester, Howard Hawks, Rio Bravo, and the Hangout Film (Establishing Shot (Indiana University blog), 2024), https://blogs.iu.edu/establishingshot/2024/06/10/howard-hawks-rio-bravo-and-the-hangout-film/ [read directly]; Dennis Lim, A Generation Finds Its Mumble (The New York Times, 2007), https://www.indiewire.com/2007/08/nyt-a-generation-finds-its-mumble-74060/ [from a search summary]; BFI, Dazed and Confused: how Richard Linklater created the ultimate high-school experience (BFI on Letterboxd), https://letterboxd.com/bfi/story/dazed-and-confused-how-richard-linklater/ [from a search summary].
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.

  provenance: Its definition was taken from a search summary, and what it asks is held with low confidence.

## Heist (heist)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. The heist film is a subgenre of crime film focused on the planning, execution and aftermath of a significant robbery.
    What it asks of an actor. Heist films are built around a crew of specialists, each with one skill, so each actor must establish a distinct role within an ensemble.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: From search summaries: heist films include the assembly of the crew, 'disparate individual criminals, each with their own set of unique skills', each 'the best at their particular skill: safe-cracking, security system disabling, driving fast getaway vehicles'. (from a search summary; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: The source describes the narrative structure, not what it asks of actors; 'ensemble precision and cool, controlled tension' is unsourced. No interviews on heist-film acting were found.
    How its scenes are played. A crew of specialists, each actor establishing a distinct role within the ensemble, and the job at risk. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Played Between Two: The scene lives between the two of you: chemistry, contrast, a part each that only works against the other. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your presence, listening and responsiveness and emotional depth. Rehearsing on your feet: a partnership is built in the exchange, played through until each part answers the other.
    Suspense Held: A threat kept alive under every line: what’s at stake never dropped, even in the quiet. A self-tape in it is judged more on consistency and interpretation, and calls on your focus and listening and responsiveness. The breakdown, then rehearsal: suspense is held by knowing at every moment what your character fears will happen, and keeping it alive through the scene.

## Historical Drama (historical_drama)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A historical drama is a dramatic work set in the past that presents historical events and characters with varying degrees of fiction.
    What it asks of an actor. Period drama asks actors for period-accurate speech: British productions want a period form of Received Pronunciation, not the modern one.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Accents) belongs to British period productions that ask for period Received Pronunciation, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: 'There’s antique RP for period pieces. There’s mid-century RP for postwar dramas'; 'An actor who learns one version and applies it to everything will sound off target in half their roles'; for British content 'the bar for authenticity is high.' (read directly; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: This is a practitioner’s article about British productions only. Portraying real historical figures, and period manners or bearing, are unsourced here (see the Docudrama genre for portraying real people). On review (October 10, 2026), one practitioner’s article about British productions wasn’t enough to make it a requirement of the whole genre.
    How its scenes are played. Period drama asks for period-accurate speech. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Another Age’s Manners: How people of another time stood, moved, spoke and kept their place, kept up without looking like effort. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and interpretation, and calls on your physical expression, discipline and vocal expression. The breakdown and rehearsal together: the manners are researched first, then made habit on your feet.

## Historical Fiction (historical_fiction)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Historical fiction is a genre in which a fictitious plot takes place in the setting of particular real historical events.
    What it asks of an actor. A leading drama school teaches period movement as the etiquette of a period, including deportment and dance, and one period series sent its leads to dance, voice and etiquette lessons. No credible source says period fiction as a whole asks actors for dance or for heightened language.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Dancing, Heightened Language) belongs to period stories with social-dance scenes (court, Regency, costume romance) for Dancing, and to adaptations of period plays (Shakespeare, Restoration comedy) or scripts written in period verse or rhetoric for Heightened Language, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Period Movement for Actors (short course): It examines "principles and rules of behaviour in three key periods of western social history, including deportment, greetings, social interaction, clothing, and dance"; units on Elizabethan/Jacobean court behavior (Shakespeare), Restoration comedy, and the 1920s Charleston. It’s training for period work generally, chiefly for the theater. Epic Actor Training for the Bridgerton Series: "Dynevor and Page attended dancing, voice, and etiquette lessons. Altogether, the duo learned how to talk, sit, eat, and walk like 1800's upper-class society." That’s one series. (both its sources read directly, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: The only film-specific source is a casting-platform blog about one deliberately anachronistic series. A trade article on period acting (Backstage, 'What is a Period Piece?') couldn’t be opened, and wasn’t read. The convention of period Received Pronunciation, if it’s real, would be a matter for Accents, not Heightened Language.
    How its scenes are played. Period movement as the etiquette of a period: deportment, dance and voice. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Another Age’s Manners: How people of another time stood, moved, spoke and kept their place, kept up without looking like effort. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and interpretation, and calls on your physical expression, discipline and vocal expression. The breakdown and rehearsal together: the manners are researched first, then made habit on your feet.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_fiction (Wikipedia; the article treats it as a literary genre also applied to film). Source for what it asks: Royal Academy of Dramatic Art (taught by Francine Watson Coleman), Period Movement for Actors (short course) (RADA, n.d.), https://www.rada.ac.uk/short-courses/period-movement-actors/ [read directly]; Casting Frontier blog, Epic Actor Training for the Bridgerton Series (Casting Frontier, 2021), https://castingfrontier.com/blog/epic-actor-training-for-the-bridgerton-series/ [read directly].

## Holiday (holiday)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
