    Its conventions. A holiday film is set during or built around a holiday season, most often Christmas or Thanksgiving, and trades on that season’s traditions and feeling.
    What it asks of an actor. Holiday films, the Hallmark kind especially, ask actors to play sentimental material with total sincerity rather than irony.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: From search summaries: 'Behind every Hallmark holiday movie lies surprising craft and skillful sincerity'; actors should 'maintain complete sincerity and earnestness, especially when delivering lines about Christmas magic' and deliver 'every cutesy holiday metaphor like they absolutely mean it.' (from a search summary; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: The article wasn’t read directly; it covers Hallmark’s formula, not holiday film in general. Sincerity isn’t one of the requirements the game can ask for. No source shows comic timing as something the genre asks, so the research didn’t name Comic Timing.
    How its scenes are played. Sentimental material played with total sincerity, inside the romantic comedy the season hosts. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.

A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and connection, and calls on your emotional depth and presence. A self-tape in it is judged more on responsiveness and execution, and calls on your adaptability and focus. Rehearsing on your feet: timing lives in the exchange and is found by playing it through.

## Hood Film (hood_film)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.

    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read

    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Hood film is a film genre originating in the United States which features aspects of urban African American or Hispanic American culture.
    What it asks of an actor. The 'hood film is tied to hip-hop culture and casts rappers-turned-actors; the founding film cast many inexperienced local actors for authenticity.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: From a search summary: the 'hood film is identified 'by a strong connection to youth rap/hip hop culture (via soundtrack and rappers-turned-actors), contemporary urban settings ... and inner-city social and political issues'; it builds on Paula J. Massood’s Black City Cinema. Search summaries of Grokipedia, which is unreliable and wasn’t used for a requirement, say Singleton cast 'mostly inexperienced actors' and drew on vernacular speech. (from a search summary; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: Nothing credible states a performance demand. Vernacular speech appears only in unreliable sources, so the research didn’t name Accents. Rappers-turned-actors is a casting pattern, not a skill.
    How its scenes are played. It cast local, often inexperienced actors for authenticity, in a world of street status. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Caught Live: Behavior that looks unrehearsed: real listening, the next line taken from what the other person just did. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your listening and responsiveness and adaptability. Rehearsing on your feet: what looks unrehearsed is found by playing it through until nothing is set but the wants.
    Command Of The Frame: Status and threat carried in the body and the look more than the words: the person everyone in the room watches. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

## Horror (horror)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Horror films are motion pictures calculated to cause intense repugnance, fear or dread.
    What it asks of an actor. Horror asks actors for sustained physical exertion (running, crawling, struggling, some fight work), extreme vocal work such as screaming, and believable reactions to threats that are often absent or minimal on set.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Fights And Stunts) belongs to chase-and-struggle roles (see the Slasher genre’s final girl), not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: "The amount of running, crawling and struggling in horror films is unparalleled"; actors work in "extreme vocal ranges: screaming, growling, shouting and crying"; stage combat familiarity helps since "you will have some instruction...but it helps to be familiar with the base concepts"; actors must imagine, for example, "a shapeshifting demon crawling towards you down a hallway" when effects are minimal or absent. Search summaries (the Mandy and Backstage pages couldn’t be opened) also quote Maika Monroe: "the screaming and crying and running...every day is very tiresome." (read directly; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: StageMilk is an acting-advice site written by practitioners, not a textbook or a union source. Screaming is vocal stamina, which isn’t one of the requirements the game can ask for. The research didn’t name Voice-Led Performance, because the source is about screaming on camera, not performing with the voice alone. The Mandy, StarNow and Backstage articles on horror acting couldn’t be opened, and weren’t read. On review (October 10, 2026), one advice site generalizing exertion and some fight work to all horror wasn’t enough to make it a requirement of the whole genre.
    How its scenes are played. Sustained exertion, screaming, and believable reactions to threats that are often absent. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    States At The Limit: Fear, grief or exhaustion at their extreme, sustained, and believed though the threat is often not in the room. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and execution, and calls on your emotional depth and physical expression. The lines, then rehearsal on your feet: an extreme state has to be found in the body, on lines you no longer think about.

## Horror Comedy (horror_comedy)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Comedy horror is a film and television genre that combines elements of comedy and horror fiction.
    What it asks of an actor. Horror comedy asks actors to play the horror element completely straight while the comedy comes from character and situation, so the cast must hold comic timing without winking at the threat.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Comedy (yours: 57.7 of 90) . This follows from how the genre is defined, not from a source that says so. A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: Pegg describes the film as a romantic comedy colliding with a zombie film; the comedy (not the horror) is where the comic register sits. The genre’s cited definition calls for comedy too.
    What the research says: Pegg: "the zombie element is completely true, and we don’t ever - they’re never comic"; "the zombie element itself is done with reverence and good faith"; the film isn’t a parody but "like another film has crashed into a romantic comedy", with the romantic-comedy side being what is sent up. (read directly; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: These are one film’s makers speaking about their own approach; generalizing 'play the horror straight' to the whole genre is an inference. Other horror comedies, splatter comedies for one, may play the gore for laughs. The IndieWire oral history was behind a paywall, and wasn’t read.
    How its scenes are played. The horror played completely straight while the cast holds its comic timing. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Played Straight: Meant completely: the feeling played with full conviction, never winked at, however large or strange the material. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and connection, and calls on your emotional depth and presence. The breakdown and the lines: believing it starts with knowing exactly what your character wants and why it matters to them.
    Comic Rhythm: Setup and payoff: cues picked up, pauses placed, the laugh landing where the timing puts it. A self-tape in it is judged more on responsiveness and execution, and calls on your adaptability and focus. Rehearsing on your feet: timing lives in the exchange and is found by playing it through.

## Isekai (isekai)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.

    Its conventions. Isekai (‘another world’) is a subgenre of Japanese fantasy and science fiction about a person or people transported to, and having to survive in, another world.
    What it asks of an actor. Isekai is predominantly an anime and light-novel form, so the screen performer is usually a voice actor, who may have to voice a modern adult’s interior life inside a fantastical new body.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Voice-Over (yours: 54.0 of 90) . This follows from how the genre is defined, not from a source that says so. A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: The cited performer’s isekai role is voice-only animation voicing, and she describes the vocal problem of the role directly.
    What the research says: Okasaki, voice of Rimuru in That Time I Got Reincarnated as a Slime, says the challenge "was definitely to find that cute voice in expression, while at the same time making sure that 37 years of middle-aged experience came through behind the voice." The genre definition (Wikipedia, used only as a lead) lists isekai as a subgenre of 'Japanese light novels, video games, manga, and anime'. (read directly; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: This is one interview about one role. Live-action isekai exists but wasn’t researched. The 'comic asides' of the earlier research aren’t supported by anything found.

## Jidaigeki (jidaigeki)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Jidaigeki is a genre of Japanese film, television and theater set in the past, typically the Edo period, with an established set of dramatic conventions.
    What it asks of an actor. Jidaigeki uses a contrived, formal, archaic register of Japanese speech, stock makeup and catchphrases, and stylized sword fights.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Classical Text (yours: 51.4 of 90) , Stage Combat (yours: 54.6 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: The page describes a formal, archaic stylized speech register as a convention. Sword fights are a standing convention; chambara is the sword-fighting branch.
    What the research says: 'A contrived form of old-fashioned Japanese speech, using modern pronunciation and grammar with a high degree of formality and frequent archaisms'; heroes wear eye makeup; protagonists deliver set catchphrases (kimarizerifu); sword fights follow set conventions, and chambara films use stylized violence. (read directly; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: The page was read through an automatic summary. It doesn’t say how much of the swordplay the lead actors do themselves.
    How its scenes are played. A contrived, formal, archaic register of speech, stock makeup and stylized fights. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Another Age’s Manners: How people of another time stood, moved, spoke and kept their place, kept up without looking like effort. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and interpretation, and calls on your physical expression, discipline and vocal expression. The breakdown and rehearsal together: the manners are researched first, then made habit on your feet.
    Played Large: Feeling worn on the outside and sized to fill the frame: the voice and the body carry it as much as the face. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and connection, and calls on your vocal expression, physical expression and presence. Rehearsing on your feet: a size the body and the voice can carry has to be found aloud and standing.

## Jukebox Musical (jukebox_musical)

    Its conventions. A jukebox musical is a stage musical or musical film whose featured songs are mostly already well-known popular songs, not an original score.
    What it asks of an actor. A jukebox musical asks actors to sing well-known popular songs as character, and in biographical cases performers report deliberately avoiding impersonation of the original artist.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Singing (yours: 53.7 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.

    What the research says: Bockel: "I’m not playing her as a person because this is a musicalized version"; she set aside YouTube research on King to avoid seeming like an impersonation; "I’m just myself with an accent." A critic quoted says she "sings with an appealing and versatile voice that echoes King’s own soulful vocals without straying into mimicry." Its definition (from search summaries of Wikipedia and dictionaries): a stage musical or film whose featured songs are mostly already well-known popular songs. (read directly; medium confidence).

The 'accent' Bockel mentions belongs to a biographical role, and doesn’t make Accents a requirement of the genre.
    How its scenes are played. Well-known songs sung as character, meant, not impersonating the original artist. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.

    Its conventions. Kaiju (‘strange beast’) eiga is a subgenre of Japanese monster film featuring giant creatures, typically attacking cities.
    What it asks of an actor. Japanese kaiju films used suit actors performing inside stiff latex or rubber creature suits on miniature sets.

    What the research says: Tsuburaya 'decided to attempt to create suits, called creature suits, for a human (suit actor) to wear and act in'; 'Due to the extreme stiffness of the latex or rubber suits, filming would often be done at double speed'; combined with miniature city sets. (read directly; low confidence).

## Kitchen Sink Drama (kitchen_sink)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Kitchen sink realism is a British cultural movement of the late 1950s and early 1960s depicting working-class lives, often through disillusioned ‘angry young men’.
    What it asks of an actor. Kitchen sink films of the British New Wave cast unknown regional actors and kept broad regional accents intact, breaking with earlier British films whose characters spoke in the accents of the educated London classes.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Accents And Dialects (yours: 52.1 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: Two independent, credible sources (BFI, and Hanley building on John Hill’s scholarship) state that regional accents and regional actors were a defining convention of the cycle as a whole, not of one film.
    What the research says: Where to begin with kitchen sink drama: The films 'were predominantly focused on the lives of the working classes, with broad regional accents thankfully intact', and gave us 'some of the greatest performances and most searing dialogue yet seen in British cinema'. The British New Wave and Its Sources: The New Wave opposed mainstream British film by 'opting for location shooting and the employment of unknown regional actors' (citing John Hill), where before 'almost all characters were from London and spoke in the accents of the educated classes'; Saturday Night and Sunday Morning opens with Arthur greeting the audience 'in the accents of industrial Nottinghamshire'. (both its sources read directly, reviewed October 10, 2026; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: The sources describe regional speech as a convention, often met by casting actors with those accents rather than by accent technique. The claim that the form asked for a new naturalistic acting style wasn’t sourced beyond search summaries, and is left out.
    How its scenes are played. Unknown regional actors with their own accents, breaking with the educated stage voice. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Caught Live: Behavior that looks unrehearsed: real listening, the next line taken from what the other person just did. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your listening and responsiveness and adaptability. Rehearsing on your feet: what looks unrehearsed is found by playing it through until nothing is set but the wants.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kitchen_sink_realism (Wikipedia). Source for what it asks: Neil Mitchell, Where to begin with kitchen sink drama (BFI, 2016), https://www.bfi.org.uk/features/where-begin-with-kitchen-sink-drama [read directly]; David Hanley, The British New Wave and Its Sources (Offscreen, vol. 15 no. 6, 2011), https://offscreen.com/view/british_new_wave [read directly].
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.
  ways: Accents And Dialects (What it asks for: Accents) | Listening And Responsiveness (Its scenes call on it: Caught Live) | Adaptability (Its scenes call on it: Caught Live)
  provenance: Its definition was read at the source, and what it asks is held with medium confidence.

    What it asks of an actor. Magic realism as a narrative mode presents fantastic elements in a matter-of-fact way within realistic fiction. No credible source found states that this translates into a particular acting register for film.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research holds this (Naturalism On Camera) with low confidence, so the game doesn’t treat it as a requirement of the genre. The small print below says why.

It’s literary, not about acting. assessed essay: representing trauma in magical realist cinema: A student essay, which couldn’t be opened: directors have actors act in a 'natural, laid-back way' without shock at the magic. It isn’t a credible source. (neither of its sources read directly, both from search summaries, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).

  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.

    Its conventions. Martial arts films are a subgenre of action film featuring martial arts combat between characters.
    What it asks of an actor. Martial-arts roles are often played by real martial artists; otherwise actors train for the role, helped by doubles, wire work and camera tricks.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Stage Combat (yours: 54.6 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: Real martial-arts skill or role-specific fight training is stated as the norm.
    What the research says: 'these roles are often played by actors who are real martial artists. If not, actors frequently train in preparation for their roles or the action director may rely more on stylized action or film making tricks'; techniques include 'doubles, undercranking, wire work'. (read directly; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: Wikipedia was used only as a lead.
    How its scenes are played. Played by martial artists, or actors trained to look like them: discipline and status in the body. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Command Of The Frame: Status and threat carried in the body and the look more than the words: the person everyone in the room watches. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.

## Medical Drama (medical_drama)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A medical drama is a film or television program whose events center on a hospital, clinic, doctor’s office or other medical setting.
    What it asks of an actor. Medical drama asks actors to learn convincing procedural business (suturing, auscultation, intubation) and to master dense medical jargon, typically through a pre-production medical boot camp and drilled repetition.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: Adelaide Kane: the newcomers "started out with a two week boot camp"; "They taught us how to suture and how to check heartbeats... how to listen for breath sounds and how to intubate people." Harry Shum Jr. on jargon: the approach was "repetition"; "We stumble a lot." (read directly; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: This is one show’s practice. None of the requirements the game can ask for covers technical jargon or procedural business, so none applies. 'High-stakes emotion', from the earlier research, isn’t addressed by the source.
    How its scenes are played. Convincing procedural business and dense jargon, drilled at a medical boot camp, inside a team. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Dense Text, Exact: A lot of words, or difficult ones, delivered fluently and at speed: jargon, argument or deduction as second nature. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and interpretation, and calls on your memory and focus. Learning the lines: fluency at speed is the lines learned past knowing them.
    Played Between Two: The scene lives between the two of you: chemistry, contrast, a part each that only works against the other. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your presence, listening and responsiveness and emotional depth. Rehearsing on your feet: a partnership is built in the exchange, played through until each part answers the other.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.

## Melodrama (melodrama)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Melodrama is a sentimental dramatic form that emphasizes exciting incidents and strong emotion, with stock characters in an improbable plot, over character development.
    What it asks of an actor. Film melodrama uses exaggerated staging, score and performance; characters wear their hearts on their sleeves.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: Melodrama 'employs exaggerated staging, score and performance to create the ultimate spectacle'; 'a passionate purge of emotion'; 'heightened dramatics'; characters 'rarely behave rationally'. (read directly; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: The source supports exaggerated emotional performance, but Heightened Language means heightened or stylized language, which the BFI doesn’t mention. None of the requirements the game can ask for fits, and none was forced.
    How its scenes are played. Exaggerated performance: characters wear their hearts on their sleeves, and mean it. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Played Large: Feeling worn on the outside and sized to fill the frame: the voice and the body carry it as much as the face. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and connection, and calls on your vocal expression, physical expression and presence. Rehearsing on your feet: a size the body and the voice can carry has to be found aloud and standing.
    Played Straight: Meant completely: the feeling played with full conviction, never winked at, however large or strange the material. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and connection, and calls on your emotional depth and presence. The breakdown and the lines: believing it starts with knowing exactly what your character wants and why it matters to them.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.

## Military (military)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A military film centers on armed forces and military life. It’s closest to the war film, about warfare, with combat scenes at the center of the drama.
    What it asks of an actor. Military and war films commonly put actors through a pre-shoot boot camp run by a military adviser, covering weapons handling, gear, squad tactics and physical hardship, to make soldiering second nature.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Fights And Stunts) belongs to combat films with an adviser’s boot camp (see the War genre), not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Ron Livingston: the camp included "weapons training, how to handle military gear and other squad-based tactics" that needed to "become second nature"; Dye allowed "up to two hours of sleep a night and two meals a day"; Dye: he "breaks actors down and builds them back up." Search summaries of Variety (not opened) add that Saving Private Ryan’s cast did 10 days of pre-dawn reveille, five-mile runs and weapons training. (read directly; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: The evidence is from one adviser’s films (the era of Platoon, Saving Private Ryan and Band of Brothers); peacetime 'military life' films without combat weren’t researched. Drill and bearing as such aren’t named in the source that was read. On review (October 10, 2026), one adviser’s films weren’t enough to make it a requirement of the whole genre.
    How its scenes are played. Boot camps make soldiering second nature: rank, and physical hardship. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Command Of The Frame: Status and threat carried in the body and the look more than the words: the person everyone in the room watches. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.
    States At The Limit: Fear, grief or exhaustion at their extreme, sustained, and believed though the threat is often not in the room. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and execution, and calls on your emotional depth and physical expression. The lines, then rehearsal on your feet: an extreme state has to be found in the body, on lines you no longer think about.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_film (Wikipedia’s war film, the nearest genre: no entry for 'military film' was found). Source for what it asks: Aaron Kassraie, 'Dale Dye: Hollywood's Go-To Military Advisor', AARP, https://www.aarp.org/veterans/hollywood-film-industry-military-adviser/.
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.
  ways: Presence (Its scenes call on it: Command Of The Frame) | Physical Expression (Its scenes call on it: Command Of The Frame, States At The Limit) | Emotional Depth (Its scenes call on it: States At The Limit)

    Still uncertain: Deadpan naturalism wasn’t stated outright, so the research didn’t name Naturalism On Camera. The page didn’t establish that mockumentaries are always comic, so it didn’t name Comic Timing either.
    How its scenes are played. Partly or wholly improvised, through documentary devices, for laughs. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.

A self-tape in it is judged more on responsiveness and execution, and calls on your adaptability and focus. Rehearsing on your feet: timing lives in the exchange and is found by playing it through.

## Monster Movie (monster_movie)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A monster movie is a film that focuses on characters struggling to survive attacks by one or more antagonistic monsters.
    What it asks of an actor. Monster movies have a tradition of the creature itself being played by a performer in a suit, which demands heavy physical endurance and studied non-human movement.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Physical Transformation) belongs to the performer in the creature suit, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Nakajima: "the temperature of my suits reached 60 degrees Celsius"; the 1954 suit weighed about 100 kg; "I watched the movement of bears"; "you cannot complain because you are a professional actor; you have to act like there is no pain." The Dartmouth guide notes Godzilla as "actor Haruo Nakajima in a rubber suit" and classes monster movies as a horror subgenre (Oxford Dictionary of Film Studies). (read directly; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: It covers the creature performer, not the human leads. The earlier claim about reacting to a creature added later in effects was supported only by search summaries about other kinds of film (Casper, Hobbit), and isn’t used. Suit work is one tradition (kaiju); many modern monster films use CGI. On review (October 10, 2026), a demand on the creature performer alone wasn’t enough to make it a requirement of the whole genre.
    How its scenes are played. The creature played in a suit with studied non-human movement; the others in terror of it. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    States At The Limit: Fear, grief or exhaustion at their extreme, sustained, and believed though the threat is often not in the room. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and execution, and calls on your emotional depth and physical expression. The lines, then rehearsal on your feet: an extreme state has to be found in the body, on lines you no longer think about.
    Belief In What Isn’t There: An invented world made concrete: the creature, the machine or the magic played as specifically as a cup of tea. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and interpretation, and calls on your imagination. The breakdown: the world’s rules have to be worked out before they can be believed.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monster_movie (Wikipedia). Source for what it asks: Haruo Nakajima interview, 'MR. GODZILLA SPEAKS!', Vantage Point Interviews (May 18, 2017), https://vantagepointinterviews.com/2017/05/18/mr-godzilla-speaks-suit-actor-haruo-nakajima-on-playing-the-king-of-the-monsters/; Dartmouth Library, 'Monsters in motion pictures', https://researchguides.dartmouth.edu/filmgenres/monsters.
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.

    Its conventions. Mumblecore is a subgenre of low-budget independent film characterized by naturalistic acting, sometimes improvised dialogue, and a focus on young adults’ personal relationships.
    What it asks of an actor. Naturalism in performance and dialogue is a key feature of almost all mumblecore; early films used non-professionals and some used prominent improvisation.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for On Camera (yours: 51.9 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: Naturalistic performance is called a key feature of almost all mumblecore films.
    What the research says: 'Naturalism – both in performance and dialogue – is a key feature of almost all mumblecore films'; 'Early mumblecore films tended to feature non-professional actors'; 'Some mumblecore films feature a prominent use of improvisation, with the cast sharing script credits'; Bujalski’s are 'mostly scripted'. (read directly; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: Improvisation is in 'some' films, not all; the page notes exceptions that are mostly scripted. The review of October 10, 2026 took Improvisation away for that reason: the convention is the naturalism, Naturalism On Camera.
    How its scenes are played. Naturalism in performance and dialogue is a key feature, with non-professionals and improvisation. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.

A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your listening and responsiveness and adaptability. Rehearsing on your feet: what looks unrehearsed is found by playing it through until nothing is set but the wants.

## Mystery (mystery)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A mystery is a narrative genre centered on unraveling the unknown, typically withholding the explanation of a puzzle or crime until the end.
    What it asks of an actor. A source on a modern whodunit says the form wants heightened, larger-than-life suspects who still read as real people; nothing found supports a distinct technique for withholding secrets.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: Johnson: "you need to get great actors and actors who can do performances that are this big but still feel like real people." He also says "all of the funniest lines in the movie, Michael just shouted out on the day." A separate search summary (Screen Rant, not opened) says Johnson gives actors the full script including the killer’s identity. (read directly; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: This is one director on one comic whodunit. Nothing found says what mystery asks of actors generally; 'withholding and layering' remains an unsourced reading. Shannon’s ad-libs don’t establish improvisation as a convention of the form.
    How its scenes are played. A modern whodunit wants heightened, larger-than-life suspects who still read as real people. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Played Large: Feeling worn on the outside and sized to fill the frame: the voice and the body carry it as much as the face. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and connection, and calls on your vocal expression, physical expression and presence. Rehearsing on your feet: a size the body and the voice can carry has to be found aloud and standing.
    Suspense Held: A threat kept alive under every line: what’s at stake never dropped, even in the quiet. A self-tape in it is judged more on consistency and interpretation, and calls on your focus and listening and responsiveness. The breakdown, then rehearsal: suspense is held by knowing at every moment what your character fears will happen, and keeping it alive through the scene.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll read Trifles, by Susan Glaspell, a play of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.

## Neo-Noir (neo_noir)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Neo-noir is a film genre from the 1970s New Hollywood era onward that revives and subverts the visual style and tropes of classic film noir.
    What it asks of an actor. Film reference works name first-person voice-over narration as a convention of classic film noir. No credible source found says neo-noir keeps it as a convention; neo-noir is described by its self-consciousness and nostalgia for noir, and many neo-noirs have no narrator.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Voice-Led Performance) belongs to the narrating lead in neo-noirs that keep classic noir’s first-person voice-over, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Film noir (research guide, quoting A Dictionary of Film Studies, Kuhn and Westwell, Oxford, 2020): It quotes Kuhn and Westwell: noir is a cycle of 1940-1959 Hollywood crime films with "dreamlike first-person voice-over narration, flashbacks, temporal ellipses, and often ambiguous endings." It gives no definition of neo-noir. Film Noir: It uses Double Indemnity’s narrator voice-over as an example of classic noir, and says "neo-noir films display a self-consciousness alien to earlier ones" and a "conflicted nostalgia" for the postwar era. It says nothing on voice-over in neo-noir. (both its sources read directly, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: No source about neo-noir performance was found. Andrew Spicer (Film Noir, Longman, 2002) was seen only through a summary noting that noir devices such as voice-over appear in non-noir films too. 'Deadpan' delivery, 'cool understatement' and 'controlled menace' remain unsupported.
    How its scenes are played. Classic noir’s cynicism revived and turned on itself: cool surfaces over dread. The research found nothing on how it’s played, so this is estimated from its conventions.
    Feeling Held In: The feeling is there and kept under: stillness, economy, what isn’t said carrying what is. A self-tape in it is judged more on consistency and believability, and calls on your composure and focus. Learning the lines and rehearsing on your feet: control comes from knowing it well enough to hold it still.
    Suspense Held: A threat kept alive under every line: what’s at stake never dropped, even in the quiet. A self-tape in it is judged more on consistency and interpretation, and calls on your focus and listening and responsiveness. The breakdown, then rehearsal: suspense is held by knowing at every moment what your character fears will happen, and keeping it alive through the scene.

## Neorealism (neorealism)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.

    Its conventions. Italian Neorealism was a postwar film movement known for realistic treatment of social themes, real locations, documentary-like objectivity and nonprofessional actors in leading roles.
    What it asks of an actor. Italian neorealism was filmed on location among the poor and working class, frequently with non-professional actors.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Naturalism On Camera) belongs to non-professional casting, a choice of who’s cast rather than a technique asked of an actor, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: A search summary of Wikipedia: 'a style of film characterized by stories set amongst the poor and working class, filmed on location, frequently using nonprofessional actors'; where known actors were cast they played 'strongly against their normal character types'. (from a search summary; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: The Britannica page couldn’t be opened, and searches returned Wikipedia and others instead. Naturalism On Camera is inferred from casting and location practice; no source here states a performance style in so many words. On review (October 10, 2026), an inference wasn’t enough to make it a requirement of the whole genre.

## New Wave (new_wave)

    Its conventions. The New Wave (Nouvelle Vague) was the style of a group of highly individualistic French directors of the late 1950s, marked by location shooting, handheld cameras and elliptical editing.
    What it asks of an actor. The New Wave promoted a naturalistic style with location shooting, improvised dialogue and natural light.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Improvisation (yours: 52.0 of 90) , On Camera (yours: 51.9 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: Improvised dialogue is named as a convention. The movement is described as promoting a naturalistic style.
    What the research says: Search summary attributed to Britannica: 'The New Wave movement promoted a more naturalistic filmmaking style, with location shoots, improvised dialogue, and natural lighting.' (from a search summary; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: The Britannica page couldn’t be opened, and the search didn’t return the New Wave page itself, so the attribution is unconfirmed, and this is secondhand.
    How its scenes are played. A naturalistic style with improvised dialogue, by highly individualistic directors. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.

    Its conventions. Nordic noir is a genre of crime fiction, usually told from a police point of view and set in Scandinavia or the Nordic countries.
    What it asks of an actor. The best-known Nordic noir lead describes a performance built from tight control of a closed, socially difficult character; the genre is characterized as slow and melancholic, but nothing found ties a specific acting style to the genre.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: Helin: "At first I regarded her as a blank wall, but then that blank wall became a glass wall and I was able to see through it"; she chose to make it look like Saga "was forcing herself to do eye contact"; "I usually have to keep Saga within certain parameters." Search summaries (of genre sites, not opened) describe Nordic noir’s "slow and melancholic pace". (read directly; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: This is one character in one series; Saga’s restraint is a character trait, not evidence of a style across the genre. No scholarly source on Nordic noir performance was found. Bilingual productions (The Bridge) suggest language demands but no source said so.

## Occult (occult)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A subgenre of horror, and at times of other genres, built around the Devil, witchcraft, demonic possession, exorcism and other hidden supernatural forces.
    What it asks of an actor. Possession and exorcism films have asked for extreme vocal and makeup work for the possessed role, as in The Exorcist, where a separate voice actress (Mercedes McCambridge) built the demon’s voice under punishing conditions and the possessed actress wore prosthetics applied for hours daily. These are role-specific to possession films, not conventions of occult horror.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Voice-Led Performance, Physical Transformation) belongs to the possessed role, and the voice of the possessing entity, in possession and exorcism films, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: The Actor Behind the Demon’s Voice in The Exorcist Went to Hell and Back: McCambridge voiced the demon, insisting on 'being tightly bound to a chair, swallowing raw eggs and Jack Daniel’s while smoking'; Friedkin layered the recordings. The article doesn’t mention Linda Blair’s makeup. Why Linda Blair Was Never The Same After The Exorcist: Blair spent at least two hours, sometimes up to five, in the makeup chair daily for Dick Smith’s prosthetics. It’s an entertainment site of little authority, read only as a search summary. (one of its two sources read directly and the other from a search summary, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: It’s a single film. Searches for a source describing possession-film acting as a convention returned only listicles. Occult horror also covers witchcraft, cults and séance films with no possession role.
    How its scenes are played. Possession roles have asked for extreme vocal and makeup work. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    States At The Limit: Fear, grief or exhaustion at their extreme, sustained, and believed though the threat is often not in the room. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and execution, and calls on your emotional depth and physical expression. The lines, then rehearsal on your feet: an extreme state has to be found in the body, on lines you no longer think about.
    Played Large: Feeling worn on the outside and sized to fill the frame: the voice and the body carry it as much as the face. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and connection, and calls on your vocal expression, physical expression and presence. Rehearsing on your feet: a size the body and the voice can carry has to be found aloud and standing.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

## Parody (parody)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A work that imitates the style and conventions of another work, author or genre, usually to ridicule them, sometimes in admiration or as a comic exercise.
    What it asks of an actor. Screen parody of the Airplane! school asks actors to deliver absurd material with complete dramatic seriousness, deadpan, oblivious to the gags around them.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Comedy (yours: 57.7 of 90) . This follows from how the genre is defined, not from a source that says so. A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: The A.V. Club credits the comedy to Nielsen’s deadpan delivery of ridiculous lines, which is verbal comic timing played straight.
    What the research says: Nielsen could invest "the most ridiculous lines of dialogue with a delivery that was serious as a heart attack"; the Zucker-Abrahams-Zucker team cast him as "an archetypal straight man oblivious to the constant stream of gags going on around him." A search summary of Nielsen’s 1993 Fresh Air interview (NPR; the page didn’t load): approaching the material "with the same seriousness and the same gravity" as their police shows "would be very funny." (read directly; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: This is one school of parody, Zucker-Abrahams-Zucker’s. Parody that relies on mimicry of a specific performer (sketch parody) wasn’t researched. 'Precise imitation of a recognisable style' isn’t directly supported.
    How its scenes are played. Absurd material delivered with complete dramatic seriousness, deadpan, oblivious to the gags. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Played Straight: Meant completely: the feeling played with full conviction, never winked at, however large or strange the material. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and connection, and calls on your emotional depth and presence. The breakdown and the lines: believing it starts with knowing exactly what your character wants and why it matters to them.
    Comic Rhythm: Setup and payoff: cues picked up, pauses placed, the laugh landing where the timing puts it. A self-tape in it is judged more on responsiveness and execution, and calls on your adaptability and focus. Rehearsing on your feet: timing lives in the exchange and is found by playing it through.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.

## Period Drama (period_drama)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.

    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read

    Its conventions. A film or television production set in a past period, marked by attention to the costume, setting and language of that era.
    What it asks of an actor. Period dramas employ historical advisers who teach actors how to speak, stand, sit, eat and gesture in the manner of the era.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: From search summaries: Alastair Bruce advised Downton Abbey actors 'on how to speak, stand, sit, eat, drink, dress', posture and 'the use of vocabulary'; Lily James: 'The main thing I had to learn was posture'. DataCalculus (read directly) says dialect coaches evoke 'the culture, education, and social class of the period'. (from a search summary; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: The earlier source, DataCalculus, is a data-analytics company’s blog and too weak to support a requirement, so the research didn’t name Accents. The Downton evidence is one series. The review of October 10, 2026 took Heightened Language away: it’s verse and rhetoric, and the research is about period speech, bearing and manners in one series, which it doesn’t fit.
    How its scenes are played. Historical advisers teach actors to speak, stand, sit, eat and gesture as in the era. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Another Age’s Manners: How people of another time stood, moved, spoke and kept their place, kept up without looking like effort. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and interpretation, and calls on your physical expression, discipline and vocal expression. The breakdown and rehearsal together: the manners are researched first, then made habit on your feet.

## Picaresque (picaresque)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.

    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A narrative, originally a Spanish novel form, relating the episodic adventures of a lowborn rogue who drifts from place to place and through social milieus in order to survive.
    What it asks of an actor. The model screen picaresque was carried by its lead’s charm across an episodic, roistering narrative; nothing found generalizes this into a performance demand.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: From a search summary: Tom Jones is "a spirited picaresque"; "Albert Finney gives a career-defining performance as the foundling whose easy charm seems to lead him astray at every turn." Another search summary (its source unclear) mentions "asides to the screen." (from a search summary; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: It’s one film, from a search summary only. There’s no source on what picaresque asks of actors generally. 'Shifting register across settings' is unsupported. The 'asides to the screen' line couldn’t be attributed, and wasn’t used for a requirement.
    How its scenes are played. The model screen picaresque was carried by its lead’s charm across an episodic, roistering story. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Command Of The Frame: Status and threat carried in the body and the look more than the words: the person everyone in the room watches. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.
    Comic Rhythm: Setup and payoff: cues picked up, pauses placed, the laugh landing where the timing puts it. A self-tape in it is judged more on responsiveness and execution, and calls on your adaptability and focus. Rehearsing on your feet: timing lives in the exchange and is found by playing it through.

## Police Procedural (police_procedural)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A subgenre of detective fiction that emphasizes an accurate depiction of the investigative procedures of police officers or law-enforcement agencies.
    What it asks of an actor. Police procedurals train their casts in real procedure (classroom instruction, traffic stops, cuffing, clearing a house, defensive tactics, ride-alongs) so the actions read as second nature on camera.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: Cudlitz (who played John Cooper on Southland): "We started in a classroom covering many of the things we would be required to do on film as cops"; training covered car positioning at traffic stops, cuffing, clearing a house and defensive tactics, ending in a ride-along with an on-duty officer; the aim was for procedure to become "second nature." (read directly; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: This is one show, in an article by one of its actors. Defensive tactics are mentioned, but not as sustained fight work, so Fights And Stunts doesn’t apply. 'Restraint in interrogation' is unsupported. None of the requirements the game can ask for covers fluency in procedure.
    How its scenes are played. Casts trained in real procedure so the actions read as second nature, working as a squad. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Dense Text, Exact: A lot of words, or difficult ones, delivered fluently and at speed: jargon, argument or deduction as second nature. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and interpretation, and calls on your memory and focus. Learning the lines: fluency at speed is the lines learned past knowing them.
    Played Between Two: The scene lives between the two of you: chemistry, contrast, a part each that only works against the other. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your presence, listening and responsiveness and emotional depth. Rehearsing on your feet: a partnership is built in the exchange, played through until each part answers the other.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_procedural (Wikipedia). Source for what it asks: Michael Cudlitz, 'Ride-Along (I'm Not a Cop, But I Look Like One on TV)', HuffPost, https://www.huffpost.com/entry/southland-preview_b_1211491.
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.

## Political Drama (political_drama)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A play, film or television program with a political component, whether reflecting the author’s political opinion or depicting a politician or a series of political events.
    What it asks of an actor. Political drama in the Sorkin mold asks actors to master fast, dense, exact dialogue through heavy repetition so it becomes subconscious, often delivered on the move.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: Whitford: actors must "learn your lines as fast as you can" and do "endless repetitions" to make dialogue "subconscious, so that technical aspect of the performance was removed"; Sorkin writes "racing, like, just ahead of the audience." A search summary (Cheat Sheet, not opened): Martin Sheen said actors could suggest but not improvise, and Sorkin wanted lines verbatim because he wrote 'in meter'. (read directly; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: This is one writer’s style on one show, not the genre. Heightened Language was considered (the 'meter' claim), but that rests on a search summary and on one writer, so the research didn’t name it. 'Quiet calculation of characters wielding power' is unsupported.
    How its scenes are played. Fast, dense, exact dialogue mastered by repetition, often delivered on the move. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Dense Text, Exact: A lot of words, or difficult ones, delivered fluently and at speed: jargon, argument or deduction as second nature. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and interpretation, and calls on your memory and focus. Learning the lines: fluency at speed is the lines learned past knowing them.
    Command Of The Frame: Status and threat carried in the body and the look more than the words: the person everyone in the room watches. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll read Major Barbara, by Bernard Shaw, a play of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_drama (Wikipedia). Source for what it asks: Screen Rant, 'The West Wing's Bradley Whitford Explains Aaron Sorkin's Speedy Dialogue', https://screenrant.com/bradley-whitford-west-wing-aaron-sorkin-dialogue/.
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.
  ways: Memory (Its scenes call on it: Dense Text, Exact) | Focus (Its scenes call on it: Dense Text, Exact) | Presence (Its scenes call on it: Command Of The Frame) | Physical Expression (Its scenes call on it: Command Of The Frame)
  provenance: Its definition was taken from a search summary, and what it asks is held with low confidence.

## Political Thriller (political_thriller)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A thriller set against the backdrop of a political power struggle, with high stakes and suspense at the core of the story.
    What it asks of an actor. A leading conspiracy-thriller performance was built on stillness and near-invisibility rather than display; nothing found makes this a stated genre convention.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: Oldman cites le Carré’s description of Smiley as a "swift" that can regulate its temperature to the room, "Almost like a chameleon", and says that passage "was where the stillness comes from"; he and Tomas Alfredson avoided details that would make Smiley memorable. (read directly; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: Tinker Tailor is usually classed as a spy thriller, so it’s a neighboring example, not a clean political-thriller one. Oldman doesn’t use the words paranoia or restraint. No source on the genre as a whole was found.
    How its scenes are played. A leading performance built on stillness and near-invisibility, not display. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Feeling Held In: The feeling is there and kept under: stillness, economy, what isn’t said carrying what is. A self-tape in it is judged more on consistency and believability, and calls on your composure and focus. Learning the lines and rehearsing on your feet: control comes from knowing it well enough to hold it still.
    Suspense Held: A threat kept alive under every line: what’s at stake never dropped, even in the quiet. A self-tape in it is judged more on consistency and interpretation, and calls on your focus and listening and responsiveness. The breakdown, then rehearsal: suspense is held by knowing at every moment what your character fears will happen, and keeping it alive through the scene.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_thriller (Wikipedia). Source for what it asks: The Film Stage, 'Tinker, Tailor, Soldier, Spy Star Gary Oldman Talks His Subtle Performance...', https://thefilmstage.com/interview-tinker-tailor-soldier-spy-star-gary-oldman-talks-his-subtle-performance-james-bond-more/.
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.
  ways: Composure (Its scenes call on it: Feeling Held In) | Focus (Its scenes call on it: Feeling Held In, Suspense Held) | Listening And Responsiveness (Its scenes call on it: Suspense Held)
  provenance: Its definition was taken from a search summary, and what it asks is held with low confidence.

## Post-Apocalyptic (post_apocalyptic)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Speculative fiction set in a world or civilization after a catastrophe such as nuclear war, plague or other disaster has caused its collapse.
    What it asks of an actor. Leads in two post-apocalyptic films lost around 30 pounds for survival-worn looks (Hardy, Mad Max: Fury Road; Mortensen, The Road), and Hardy planned months of daily fight training. These are particular productions, not genre conventions.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Fights And Stunts, Physical Transformation) belongs to action-led post-apocalyptic films (the Mad Max line) for Fights And Stunts, and to particular leads playing starving survivors for Physical Transformation, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Tom Hardy Interview MAD MAX: FURY ROAD; Training, His Look, Shooting Schedule, Filming with George Miller: Hardy: 'I’m meant to drop 30 pounds... I’ve already dropped it'; 'I’ll probably get three or four months of fight training out there. Seven days a week.' Viggo Mortensen Interview THE ROAD: Mortensen: 'from the clothes that didn’t fit, I think it was around maybe 30 pounds'; 'I just ate a lot less.' (both its sources read directly, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: Searches for genre conventions found only student slides and listicles (wasteland settings, scavenging, survival), nothing credible on what the genre asks of actors. The 'little dialogue' claim about Fury Road stays unconfirmed.
    How its scenes are played. Leads lost weight for survival-worn looks and trained daily for the fights. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    States At The Limit: Fear, grief or exhaustion at their extreme, sustained, and believed though the threat is often not in the room. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and execution, and calls on your emotional depth and physical expression. The lines, then rehearsal on your feet: an extreme state has to be found in the body, on lines you no longer think about.
    Command Of The Frame: Status and threat carried in the body and the look more than the words: the person everyone in the room watches. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

## Prison Film (prison_film)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A film genre concerned with prison life and often with prison escape.
    What it asks of an actor. Some prison films build performance from authenticity: casting formerly incarcerated people and shooting improvised, documentary-feel ensemble scenes, or shooting in real former prisons.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: "More than 85 per cent of Sing Sing’s cast is made up of formerly incarcerated men playing versions of themselves"; rehearsal scenes were "largely improvised and shot via Steadicam, giving them a documentary feel"; the directors "merely provided prompts." Search summaries say Starred Up was shot in a former Belfast prison with ex-convicts. (read directly; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: Sing Sing’s method is unusual, so Improvisation isn’t a convention of the genre, and the research didn’t name it. 'Psychological toll of long confinement' is unsupported. No source on the genre as a whole was found.
    How its scenes are played. Formerly incarcerated people cast, and improvised, documentary-feel ensemble scenes. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Caught Live: Behavior that looks unrehearsed: real listening, the next line taken from what the other person just did. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your listening and responsiveness and adaptability. Rehearsing on your feet: what looks unrehearsed is found by playing it through until nothing is set but the wants.
    Played Between Two: The scene lives between the two of you: chemistry, contrast, a part each that only works against the other. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your presence, listening and responsiveness and emotional depth. Rehearsing on your feet: a partnership is built in the exchange, played through until each part answers the other.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prison_film (Wikipedia). Source for what it asks: ABC News (Australia), 'Sing Sing tells a remarkable true story of a prison theatre program, starring Colman Domingo' (January 14, 2025), https://www.abc.net.au/news/2025-01-14/sing-sing-colman-domingo-interview/104791974.
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.
  ways: Listening And Responsiveness (Its scenes call on it: Caught Live, Played Between Two) | Adaptability (Its scenes call on it: Caught Live) | Presence (Its scenes call on it: Played Between Two) | Emotional Depth (Its scenes call on it: Played Between Two)

## Psychological Horror (psychological_horror)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A subgenre of horror and psychological fiction that focuses on mental, emotional and psychological states to frighten, disturb or unsettle its audience.
    What it asks of an actor. A central psychological-horror performance was played as grief and family drama pushed to extremes, with the horror grounded in something real rather than played as spectacle.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: Collette: the film is "a meditation on grief and people dealing with emotional pain"; on intense scenes, "trying to push it away from me until it was the moment between 'action' and 'cut'—resisting it and just letting it out at the right time"; she calls it and The Sixth Sense "not solely horror films." (read directly; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: It’s one film. Nothing found states 'subtlety and ambiguity rather than spectacle' as a genre demand; Collette’s performance is in fact very large in places. None of the requirements the game can ask for fits.
    How its scenes are played. Grief and family drama pushed to extremes, the horror grounded in something real. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    States At The Limit: Fear, grief or exhaustion at their extreme, sustained, and believed though the threat is often not in the room. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and execution, and calls on your emotional depth and physical expression. The lines, then rehearsal on your feet: an extreme state has to be found in the body, on lines you no longer think about.
    Played Straight: Meant completely: the feeling played with full conviction, never winked at, however large or strange the material. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and connection, and calls on your emotional depth and presence. The breakdown and the lines: believing it starts with knowing exactly what your character wants and why it matters to them.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychological_horror (Wikipedia). Source for what it asks: Time, 'Toni Collette Interview on New Horror Hit Hereditary', https://time.com/5295637/toni-collette-hereditary-horror-interview/.
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.

## Psychological Thriller (psychological_thriller)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.

    Its conventions. A genre combining the thriller and psychological fiction, dealing with characters’ mental states, perception and identity in a suspenseful setting.
    What it asks of an actor. Psychological thriller leads are often unreliable or self-concealing characters, and one leading example describes playing such a character as clinical and fully worked out in advance.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: From a search summary: Pike says Amy is "not someone overpowered by rage but driven by rage"; playing her isn’t about unleashing something animal, everything is "worked out in advance," making her "so clinical that it gives the actress a buffer." A separate search summary of NPR’s Fresh Air (Natalie Portman on Black Swan) says she trained with New York City Ballet members. (from a search summary; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: It’s from a search summary only; the page wasn’t read. These are single examples. Ballet in Black Swan belongs to that film, and doesn’t make Dancing a requirement of the genre.
    How its scenes are played. A self-concealing lead played as clinical and fully worked out in advance. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Feeling Held In: The feeling is there and kept under: stillness, economy, what isn’t said carrying what is. A self-tape in it is judged more on consistency and believability, and calls on your composure and focus. Learning the lines and rehearsing on your feet: control comes from knowing it well enough to hold it still.
    Dense Text, Exact: A lot of words, or difficult ones, delivered fluently and at speed: jargon, argument or deduction as second nature. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and interpretation, and calls on your memory and focus. Learning the lines: fluency at speed is the lines learned past knowing them.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.
