## Queer Cinema (queer_cinema)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Films involving the representation of fictional and factual lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender or queer people, storylines and community issues.
    What it asks of an actor. Queer cinema is defined by its subjects, not by a performance technique; the only performer-side source found concerns intimacy coordination on queer productions.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: Robbie Taylor Hunt: before rehearsal he asks actors individually "This is what’s in the script, what are we thinking about it? What do we want, and not want, to do?"; his work covers "simulated sex, nudity, kissing, physical touch..." and aims at the scene’s intended emotional and narrative effect. (read directly; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: Intimacy coordination is general industry practice, not specific to queer cinema, and not every queer film has intimate scenes, so the research didn’t name Staged Intimacy. 'Truthful, unstereotyped portrayal' is unsupported by any source read.
    How its scenes are played. Defined by its subjects: lives and relationships played truthfully. The research found nothing on how it’s played, so this is estimated from its conventions.
    Played Straight: Meant completely: the feeling played with full conviction, never winked at, however large or strange the material. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and connection, and calls on your emotional depth and presence. The breakdown and the lines: believing it starts with knowing exactly what your character wants and why it matters to them.
    Played Between Two: The scene lives between the two of you: chemistry, contrast, a part each that only works against the other. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your presence, listening and responsiveness and emotional depth. Rehearsing on your feet: a partnership is built in the exchange, played through until each part answers the other.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_film (Wikipedia; its New Queer Cinema article, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_queer_cinema, was read too). Source for what it asks: PinkNews, 'Intimacy co-ordinator behind major queer sex scenes reveals what their job is really like' (October 30, 2024), https://www.thepinknews.com/2024/10/30/queer-intimacy-coordinator-robbie-taylor-hunt-mr-loverman-reverberation/.
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.

## Revenge (revenge)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A film centered on vengeance, usually following a protagonist who has suffered a grievous wrong and pursues payback against those responsible.
    What it asks of an actor. Revenge action films have required their leads to undergo months of full-time martial-arts and weapons training to carry the fight work.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Fights And Stunts) belongs to revenge action films and their fight-trained leads, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Thurman trained "three solid months, five days a week, nine to five", learning "knife-throwing, a little bit of target practice. Everything from fencing to samurai style, to Wushu style." Search summaries (Maxim, not opened) say Keanu Reeves trained eight hours a day for six months for John Wick. (read directly; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: Revenge dramas without action, such as slow-burn thrillers, exist and wouldn’t need this. 'Grief turning into obsession' is unsupported. On review (October 10, 2026), training that only revenge action films ask for wasn’t enough to make it a requirement of the whole genre.
    How its scenes are played. Months of fight training to carry a protagonist driven by a grievous wrong. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    States At The Limit: Fear, grief or exhaustion at their extreme, sustained, and believed though the threat is often not in the room. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and execution, and calls on your emotional depth and physical expression. The lines, then rehearsal on your feet: an extreme state has to be found in the body, on lines you no longer think about.
    Command Of The Frame: Status and threat carried in the body and the look more than the words: the person everyone in the room watches. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

    Its conventions. A film genre in which the main characters leave home on a road trip, a journey that typically alters the perspective of their everyday lives.
    What it asks of an actor. The road movie’s in-car filming foregrounds dialogue between side-by-side travelers.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: "The physical constraints of filming in a car tend to lead to a heavy reliance on side-by-side shots and the foregrounding of dialogue"; road movies also favor "montage sequences, travelling and aerial shots, and diegetic music." (read secondhand; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: The Oxford text was read as quoted by the Dartmouth guide, not in the original. It describes the form’s shooting and dialogue, not what it asks of actors; 'gradual character change' is a narrative feature, not a performance demand. None of the requirements the game can ask for fits.
    How its scenes are played. In-car filming foregrounds dialogue between side-by-side travelers. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.

Rehearsing on your feet: a partnership is built in the exchange, played through until each part answers the other.
    Caught Live: Behavior that looks unrehearsed: real listening, the next line taken from what the other person just did. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your listening and responsiveness and adaptability. Rehearsing on your feet: what looks unrehearsed is found by playing it through until nothing is set but the wants.

## Rockumentary (rockumentary)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A documentary about rock music or rock musicians.
    What it asks of an actor. A rockumentary is a documentary about rock music, so its subjects are real musicians, not actors. Acting demands come from the mock rockumentary: This Is Spinal Tap’s dialogue was, its director says, entirely improvised, and (by secondary report) its leads played and sang the music themselves.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Improvisation, Singing) belongs to the mock rockumentary, This Is Spinal Tap and its imitators, with Improvisation for the whole cast and Singing for the actor-musicians in its band roles, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: rockumentary: "a documentary about rock music"; coined 1970-75 from rock + documentary. Rob Reiner: how we made This Is Spinal Tap: Reiner: "The entire film was improvised. The only speech that’s not improvised is Patrick Macnee." Physical gags (Stonehenge, the amp that goes to 11) were planned; dialogue wasn’t. The page that was read doesn’t quote anyone on the actors playing the music. Spinal Tap (band): "The quintet played their own instruments throughout the film." It’s uncited, and used only as a lead. Search summaries (TCM’s 'Behind the Camera', which couldn’t be opened) say Guest, McKean and Shearer played and sang the music. (all three of its sources read directly, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: The singing claim for Spinal Tap rests on Wikipedia and search summaries; TCM and Britannica couldn’t be opened. Other mock rockumentaries, such as Popstar: Never Stop Never Stopping and CB4, weren’t researched.
    How its scenes are played. The mock rockumentary’s dialogue was largely improvised, around performances played to a crowd. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Caught Live: Behavior that looks unrehearsed: real listening, the next line taken from what the other person just did. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your listening and responsiveness and adaptability. Rehearsing on your feet: what looks unrehearsed is found by playing it through until nothing is set but the wants.
    Played Large: Feeling worn on the outside and sized to fill the frame: the voice and the body carry it as much as the face. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and connection, and calls on your vocal expression, physical expression and presence. Rehearsing on your feet: a size the body and the voice can carry has to be found aloud and standing.

    Its conventions. A film genre whose narrative is organized around the pursuit of love, depicted in a lighthearted manner, and almost always resolved happily.
    What it asks of an actor. Under the Hays Code, romantic comedies relied on verbal sparring and physical comedy.

    What the research says: From a search summary: the Hays Code 'prohibited explicit mention of sexual relationships, leading to films' relying on verbal sparring and physical comedy instead'; the genre is defined by the lighthearted pursuit of love. (from a search summary; medium confidence).

## Samurai (samurai)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. Japanese films about samurai, closely tied to chanbara sword-fight films and the broader jidaigeki period drama set before the Meiji Restoration of 1868.
    What it asks of an actor. Samurai films use tate, a stage swordplay of fixed forms that actors memorize and combine, with strict posture and etiquette.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Stage Combat (yours: 54.6 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: The page describes learned, choreographed stage swordplay for film and stage.
    What the research says: Tate is 'stage fighting in brawls or with swords, in stage plays and movies' using fixed forms actors memorize and combine; it requires straight back and planted heels, proper kimono wear and sword etiquette. (read directly; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: The Gate is a Japan-culture travel site describing a tate experience, not an academic source. Period bearing isn’t exactly one of the requirements the game can ask for.
    How its scenes are played. Tate swordplay with strict posture and etiquette. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Another Age’s Manners: How people of another time stood, moved, spoke and kept their place, kept up without looking like effort. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and interpretation, and calls on your physical expression, discipline and vocal expression. The breakdown and rehearsal together: the manners are researched first, then made habit on your feet.
    Command Of The Frame: Status and threat carried in the body and the look more than the words: the person everyone in the room watches. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samurai_cinema (Wikipedia). Source for what it asks: The Gate (thegate12.com), article on tate, https://thegate12.com/article/283.
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.

## Satire (satire)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. An artistic form, chiefly literary and dramatic, in which human or individual vices, follies, abuses or shortcomings are held up to censure by ridicule, irony, parody or caricature.
    What it asks of an actor. In Dr. Strangelove, Peter Sellers played three roles, building each from voice and then walk, and improvised much of his dialogue, which Kubrick kept. This is one film and one performer’s method, not a convention of screen satire.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Physical Transformation, Improvisation) belongs to character-comedian satire (Sellers in Dr. Strangelove) and improvised character-prank satire, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Peter Sellers' defining performance in 'Dr Strangelove': Sellers: 'I start with the voice ... After that I establish how the character walks.' He played Muffley, Mandrake and Strangelove and improvised much of his dialogue, which Kubrick incorporated. Comedy: It distinguishes satire by its corrective aim ('Parody is essentially affectionate in nature, without satire’s goal of offering a corrective to behavior'), and states no acting technique for satire. (both its sources read directly, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: Far Out is a magazine, not a reference work; Britannica’s entry couldn’t be opened. The 'straight face' idea surfaced only from listicles. Satire is defined by aim, not by a performance mode, in the one reference work read.
    How its scenes are played. Vices held up to ridicule; its classic screen performance built each figure from voice and walk. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    The World’s Own Logic: A world with rules of its own, absurd, dreamlike or satirical, played wholeheartedly by them and never commented on. A self-tape in it is judged more on interpretation and execution, and calls on your imagination and physical expression. The breakdown: the world’s logic has to be found before it can be played as if it were sense.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll read The Importance Of Being Earnest, by Oscar Wilde, a play of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://www.britannica.com/art/satire. Source for what it asks: Calum Russell, Peter Sellers' defining performance in 'Dr Strangelove' (Far Out Magazine, 2021), https://faroutmagazine.co.uk/peter-sellers-stanley-kubrick-dr-strangelove/ [read directly]; Wes D. Gehring, Comedy (Schirmer Encyclopedia of Film, via Encyclopedia.com, 2007), https://www.encyclopedia.com/arts/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/comedy [read directly].

    What it asks of an actor. The slapstick comic must be acrobat and stunt performer as well as comedian, with perfect timing.

    Why it’s applied: Britannica defines slapstick as comedy of broad physical action; the comic is 'a master of uninhibited action'. Britannica says the slapstick comic 'must often be an acrobat, a stunt performer'. Britannica names 'perfect timing' as part of the slapstick comic’s mastery.

## Slice Of Life (slice_of_life)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A genre depicting ordinary people and the real drama of everyday life, often without a conventional plot or dramatic climax.
    What it asks of an actor. 'Slice of life' comes from Jean Jullien’s naturalist theater (tranche de vie) and names everyday depiction without conventional plot. No source states a naturalistic acting demand for slice-of-life film. Its canonical film master, Ozu, dictated actors’ every gesture, which runs against a naturalistic method.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research holds this (Naturalism On Camera) with low confidence, so the game doesn’t treat it as a requirement of the genre. The small print below says why.
    What the research says: There was a father: Ryu Chishu remembers Ozu Yasujiro: Ozu 'made up the complete picture in his head before he went on the set'; actors had to 'follow his directions, from the way we lifted and dropped our arms to the way we blinked our eyes'. Slice of life, used only as a lead: the term is a calque of Jullien’s tranche de vie ('A play is a slice of life put onstage with art'); in theater the term refers to naturalism. It’s a storytelling definition, not about acting. (one of its two sources read directly and the other from a search summary, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: The theater-naturalism origin is real, but whether that implies an acting technique on film is unsourced. Ozu’s results read as natural on screen, so the counter-evidence is about method, not effect.
    How its scenes are played. From naturalist theater’s tranche de vie: everyday life without a conventional plot. The research found nothing on how it’s played, so this is estimated from its conventions.
    Caught Live: Behavior that looks unrehearsed: real listening, the next line taken from what the other person just did. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your listening and responsiveness and adaptability. Rehearsing on your feet: what looks unrehearsed is found by playing it through until nothing is set but the wants.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

Source for what it asks: Ryu Chishu, There was a father: Ryu Chishu remembers Ozu Yasujiro (BFI, Sight and Sound, 1964), https://www.bfi.org.uk/sight-and-sound/interviews/there-was-father-ryu-chishu-remembers-ozu-yasujiro [read directly]; Wikipedia contributors, Slice of life (Wikipedia (pointer only)), https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slice_of_life [from a search summary].

## Social Realism (social_realism)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.

    Its conventions. Work that depicts the everyday lives and real socio-political conditions of poor and working-class people, often to critique the power structures behind them.
    What it asks of an actor. Ken Loach, the touchstone of British social realism, casts non-professionals alongside actors, withholds the full script, shoots in sequence and encourages improvisation.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Improvisation, Naturalism On Camera) belongs to Ken Loach’s productions and those that work his way, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: From search summaries: Loach uses non-professionals, limits actors’ access to the script, shoots chronologically, 'avoids extensive rehearsals and encourages actors to improvise as a way of achieving spontaneity'. (from a search summary; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: The Into Film page showed no text when opened. One director’s method stands in for the genre. On review (October 10, 2026), one director’s method wasn’t enough to make it a requirement of the whole genre.
    How its scenes are played. Non-professionals beside actors, scripts withheld, shot in sequence and improvised for spontaneity. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Caught Live: Behavior that looks unrehearsed: real listening, the next line taken from what the other person just did. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your listening and responsiveness and adaptability. Rehearsing on your feet: what looks unrehearsed is found by playing it through until nothing is set but the wants.

## Space Opera (space_opera)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A subgenre of science fiction of futuristic, melodramatic adventure set among space travelers and extraterrestrial beings, usually on a galactic scale.
    What it asks of an actor. The term space opera began as a derogatory label for hackneyed pulp spaceship yarns, coined on the model of 'horse opera' and 'soap opera', and the BFI ties the genre to an over-the-top, camp approach to storytelling. Harrison Ford’s on-set joke that Lucas’s Star Wars lines could be typed but not said concerns one film’s dialogue, not heightened or classical language.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research holds this (Heightened Language) with low confidence, so the game doesn’t treat it as a requirement of the genre. The small print below says why.
    What the research says: Space Opera: Wilson Tucker coined 'space opera' in 1941 for the "hacky, grinding, stinking, outworn, spaceship yarn", after 'horse opera' and 'soap opera'. It says nothing about dialogue or acting in space opera films. 10 great space opera films: Space opera "has long been tied to a silly, over-the-top approach to storytelling"; it cites Sontag on Flash Gordon as camp. It doesn’t discuss dialogue or acting in general terms. Harrison Ford Wasn’t Always A Fan Of George Lucas' Star Wars Writing: Ford’s line "George, you can type this s***, but you can’t say it" was an on-set joke he recounted to GQ in 2017; Lucas told Empire in 1999 that "dialogue isn’t my strength." It says nothing about verse, rhetoric or a classical register. (all three of its sources read directly, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: A broader 'stylised, sincere melodrama' playing style may be real, but it isn’t Heightened Language, and no credible acting source states it. Action and effects work weren’t researched.
    How its scenes are played. The BFI ties it to an over-the-top, camp approach among spaceships and aliens. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.

  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.

    Its conventions. A subgenre of Westerns set in the 19th-century American West but made by Italian filmmakers and shot in Europe, chiefly in the 1960s and early 1970s, known for a cynical, violent outlook and antiheroic characters.
    What it asks of an actor. Leone’s spaghetti westerns hold the actor’s face in extreme close-up with little dialogue; Eastwood played the lead with an economy of words.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Naturalism On Camera) belongs to Sergio Leone’s films, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Wikipedia (from a search summary): Leone’s style juxtaposes extreme close-ups with long shots; he 'had a fascination with faces and used little dialogue'. Remind (from a search summary): Eastwood wanted to play the Man With No Name 'with an economy of words' and felt 'the less he said, the stronger he became'. (from a search summary; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: One director stands in for the genre. Remind is a popular magazine. Neither source discusses gun or horse handling, which this genre likely shares with the Western. On review (October 10, 2026), one director’s films weren’t enough to make it a requirement of the whole genre.

A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.
    Feeling Held In: The feeling is there and kept under: stillness, economy, what isn’t said carrying what is. A self-tape in it is judged more on consistency and believability, and calls on your composure and focus. Learning the lines and rehearsing on your feet: control comes from knowing it well enough to hold it still.

## Sports (sports)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A film genre in which a sport, sporting event, athlete or follower of sport plays a prominent role and drives the plot or its resolution.
    What it asks of an actor. Sports films ask actors to train in the sport depicted so they read as believable athletes.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Fights And Stunts) belongs to combat-sport films such as boxing films, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: NPR’s headline and a search summary: the actors trained to row like the 1936 U.S. Olympic crew in order to 'appear believable as Olympic athletes'. THR grades actors on the sports skill they display on screen. (from a search summary; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: The NPR page didn’t load; only its headline and a search summary were read. Fights And Stunts is trained as Stage Combat, which fits combat sports (boxing films) better than rowing or baseball, so the fit is partial. On review (October 10, 2026), a fit with combat sports alone wasn’t enough to make it a requirement of the whole genre.
    How its scenes are played. Actors train to read as athletes; the team and the contest push them to their limit. The research found nothing on how it’s played, so this is estimated from its conventions.
    States At The Limit: Fear, grief or exhaustion at their extreme, sustained, and believed though the threat is often not in the room. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and execution, and calls on your emotional depth and physical expression. The lines, then rehearsal on your feet: an extreme state has to be found in the body, on lines you no longer think about.
    Played Between Two: The scene lives between the two of you: chemistry, contrast, a part each that only works against the other. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your presence, listening and responsiveness and emotional depth. Rehearsing on your feet: a partnership is built in the exchange, played through until each part answers the other.

    Its conventions. A modernist approach to filmmaking that arose in Paris in the 1920s, combining shocking, irrational or absurd imagery with dreamlike symbolism to challenge the idea that art should represent reality.
    What it asks of an actor. Buñuel, the defining surrealist filmmaker, told actors as little as possible and directed them mostly through physical movement and outward gesture.
    Why it adds no requirement. The research found nothing it asks beyond what any self-tape asks.
    What the research says: From a search summary: 'Buñuel told actors as little as possible, and limited his directions mostly to physical movements'; another result notes control of actors 'with special attention paid to gesture and outward appearance'. (from a search summary; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: One director stands in for the movement. The source supports an approach that isn’t psychological, but it doesn’t fit any of the requirements the game can ask for.
    How its scenes are played. Buñuel told actors little and directed them through physical movement and outward gesture. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    The World’s Own Logic: A world with rules of its own, absurd, dreamlike or satirical, played wholeheartedly by them and never commented on. A self-tape in it is judged more on interpretation and execution, and calls on your imagination and physical expression. The breakdown: the world’s logic has to be found before it can be played as if it were sense.

## Tokusatsu (tokusatsu)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.

    Its conventions. A Japanese term for live-action films or television programs that make heavy use of practical special effects, such as kaiju films and transforming-hero series.
    What it asks of an actor. Tokusatsu suit actors do their stunts and sword fights inside full costumes that hide their faces, acting through body language, with voices dubbed later.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Fights And Stunts, Physical Transformation) belongs to the suit actor, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: 'the suit actor does all of their stunts while in a full costume that normally obscures their identity'; roles 'may require extensive sword fights, actions and stunts within a physically restrictive environment'; 'extensive body language and pantomime are crucial'; 'the actor’s voice is dubbed during after-recording'. (read directly; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: It applies to suit actors, not the unmasked cast. Voice dubbing is done to the suit actor, not by them, so the research didn’t name Voice-Led Performance. On review (October 10, 2026), a demand on suit actors alone wasn’t enough to make it a requirement of the whole genre.
    How its scenes are played. Suit actors act through body language in full costume, voices dubbed later. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Played Large: Feeling worn on the outside and sized to fill the frame: the voice and the body carry it as much as the face. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.

## Tragedy (tragedy)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A branch of drama that treats in a serious and dignified style the sorrowful or terrible events encountered or caused by a heroic individual.
    What it asks of an actor. Classical tragedy is enacted in language 'enriched with ornaments' (Aristotle); Greek tragic actors wore masks and the choral parts were sung.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Heightened Language, Physical Transformation) belongs to classical verse tragedy, where a play asks for them itself (Macbeth calls for Classical Text most of all), not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Wikipedia: Aristotle defines tragedy as enacted 'by means of language enriched [with ornaments]'; 'All actors were male and wore masks'; choral parts were sung. Almeida: masks 'allowing actors to play multiple roles' with exaggerated features read from a distance; tragic actors wore raised boots. (read directly; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: Both hold for classical tragedy, Greek and in verse, not for modern prose or film tragedy. Almeida’s page didn’t mention verse when read; that came from Wikipedia’s account of Aristotle. On review (October 10, 2026), applying them to every tragedy would have asked Heightened Language of Woyzeck, Miss Julie and Riders To The Sea, so they aren’t requirements of the whole genre.
    How its scenes are played. Language enriched with ornaments, and a hero’s suffering at its full magnitude. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Played Large: Feeling worn on the outside and sized to fill the frame: the voice and the body carry it as much as the face. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and connection, and calls on your vocal expression, physical expression and presence. Rehearsing on your feet: a size the body and the voice can carry has to be found aloud and standing.
    States At The Limit: Fear, grief or exhaustion at their extreme, sustained, and believed though the threat is often not in the room. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and execution, and calls on your emotional depth and physical expression. The lines, then rehearsal on your feet: an extreme state has to be found in the body, on lines you no longer think about.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll read Woyzeck, by Georg Büchner, a play of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.

## True Crime (true_crime)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A non-fiction genre across literature, film and television that examines actual crimes, especially murder, and their investigation and prosecution.
    What it asks of an actor. Dramatized true crime casts actors as real people, and acting coaches treat matching a real person’s speech, including accent where it differs, as one important part of preparing such a role, alongside voice quality and physicality. Accent isn’t required when the person’s speech is close to the actor’s, and much true crime is documentary with no actors at all.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Accents) belongs to roles in dramatized true crime that portray real people whose speech differs from the actor’s own, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Playing a Real-Life Person as an Actor: "A lot of actors will jump straight to accent, and that is an important part of the process! But it’s not the only thing to consider." It recommends researching how the person actually spoke and moved, and doesn’t say an accent is always required. It’s about real-person roles generally, not true crime. A movie accent expert comments on 31 actors playing real people: From a search summary: a dialect coach reviews accent work by actors playing real people. It shows accent work is a recurring part of real-person roles, and says nothing about true crime as a genre. (one of its two sources read directly and the other from a search summary, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: StageMilk is an actor-training site, credible for practice but not a reference work. No trade or scholarly source on acting in dramatized true crime was found. That much true crime is documentary is common knowledge, not sourced here.
    How its scenes are played. Real people’s speech matched, and events held to the record. The research found nothing on how it’s played, so this is estimated from its conventions.
    Dense Text, Exact: A lot of words, or difficult ones, delivered fluently and at speed: jargon, argument or deduction as second nature. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and interpretation, and calls on your memory and focus. Learning the lines: fluency at speed is the lines learned past knowing them.
    Feeling Held In: The feeling is there and kept under: stillness, economy, what isn’t said carrying what is. A self-tape in it is judged more on consistency and believability, and calls on your composure and focus. Learning the lines and rehearsing on your feet: control comes from knowing it well enough to hold it still.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.
    Source for its definition: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/True_crime (Wikipedia); also https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/true%20crime (Merriam-Webster). Source for what it asks: Alexander Lee-Rekers, Playing a Real-Life Person as an Actor (StageMilk, 2022), https://www.stagemilk.com/playing-a-real-life-person-as-an-actor/ [read directly]; Boing Boing (on WIRED's 'Technique Critique' with dialect coach Erik Singer), A movie accent expert comments on 31 actors playing real people (Boing Boing, 2017), https://boingboing.net/2017/08/23/a-movie-accent-comments-on-31.html [from a search summary].
    Loving a genre makes a day of work in it better. It doesn’t make you better at it.
  ways: Memory (Its scenes call on it: Dense Text, Exact) | Focus (Its scenes call on it: Dense Text, Exact, Feeling Held In) | Composure (Its scenes call on it: Feeling Held In)
  provenance: Its definition was taken from a search summary, and what it asks is held with low confidence.

## Vampire (vampire)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it

## War (war)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A film genre concerned with warfare, typically land, sea or air battles, with combat scenes central to the drama.
    What it asks of an actor. War films routinely put casts through military boot camps run by advisers so they can play soldiers credibly.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Stage Combat (yours: 54.6 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: The sources document sustained military physical training for combat roles as standard preparation.
    What the research says: From search summaries: Dale Dye ran a 30-day military-style boot camp for the Platoon cast and an intensive one for Saving Private Ryan (sleeping in rain, 5-mile runs); his company Warriors Inc. trains actors for war films. (from a search summary; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: None of the requirements the game can ask for is exactly military drill and bearing; Fights And Stunts covers only the physical-action part. These are search summaries.
    How its scenes are played. Boot camps so casts can play soldiers credibly, as a unit under fire. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    States At The Limit: Fear, grief or exhaustion at their extreme, sustained, and believed though the threat is often not in the room. A self-tape in it is judged more on believability and execution, and calls on your emotional depth and physical expression.   summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.

    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read

    Its conventions. A genre of novels, stories, films and television set in the American West, usually between the 1850s and the end of the 19th century.
    What it asks of an actor. Western productions run 'cowboy camps' that teach actors horse riding and gun handling.
    What a self-tape in it asks. A self-tape in it calls for Stage Combat (yours: 54.6 of 90) . A night of a play of its kind, or a class scene from one, calls for its own material’s techniques instead.
    Why it’s applied: Riding and gun handling are trained physical-action skills the sources show westerns requiring; firearms handling falls within stage-combat training.
    What the research says: From search summaries: camps teach riding, mounting and dismounting, roping and firearm handling; Tim Blake Nelson trained with guns for about five months for gun tricks in The Ballad of Buster Scruggs. (from a search summary; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: The examples are mostly recent TV westerns. Horsemanship isn’t stage combat, so Fights And Stunts fits only part of it. Laconic manner wasn’t sourced.
    How its scenes are played. Cowboy camps teach riding and guns; the western’s hero says little and owns the frame. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.

A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.
    Feeling Held In: The feeling is there and kept under: stillness, economy, what isn’t said carrying what is. A self-tape in it is judged more on consistency and believability, and calls on your composure and focus. Learning the lines and rehearsing on your feet: control comes from knowing it well enough to hold it still.

## Whodunit (whodunit)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.

    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it

    Its conventions. A detective or mystery story, presented as a novel, play or film, built around discovering who committed a crime.
    What it asks of an actor. For Murder on the Orient Express (2017), Kenneth Branagh worked with a dialect coach three times a week and listened to recordings of 27 Belgian accents to play Poirot. The accent belongs to that detective role, not to the whodunit form.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Accents) belongs to foreign-detective roles such as Poirot, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: 9 Preparatory Steps Branagh Took To Play Hercule Poirot: 'He met with a dialect coach three times a week to study and practice Poirot’s accent'; he 'listened to recordings of 27 different Belgian accents'; he began learning French 'after Wallonian dialect defeated him'. (its one source read directly, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: The source is the rights-holder’s promotional site, reliable for the facts but about one film. Ensemble suspect-playing wasn’t sourced.
    How its scenes are played. Poirot’s accent built with a dialect coach, among suspects played larger than life. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Played Large: Feeling worn on the outside and sized to fill the frame: the voice and the body carry it as much as the face. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and connection, and calls on your vocal expression, physical expression and presence. Rehearsing on your feet: a size the body and the voice can carry has to be found aloud and standing.
    Dense Text, Exact: A lot of words, or difficult ones, delivered fluently and at speed: jargon, argument or deduction as second nature. A self-tape in it is judged more on execution and interpretation, and calls on your memory and focus. Learning the lines: fluency at speed is the lines learned past knowing them.

    What it asks of an actor. Wuxia films by King Hu and the Shaw Brothers used wire- and trampoline-assisted acrobatics and choreographed sword action.

    What the research says: 'sophisticated action choreography using wire and trampoline assisted acrobatics combined with sped-up camera techniques'; Yuen Woo-ping became famous as a wuxia action choreographer. (read directly; medium confidence).
    Still uncertain: The page doesn’t say how much the actors perform themselves rather than doubles.

## Yakuza (yakuza)
  summary: You haven’t studied it or worked in it yet. Study it, or tape its brief, to get to know it.
  body:
    Familiarity 0.0 Films watched and plays read 
    Work In It 0.0 Nights on stage and self-tapes in it 
    Preparation 0% Faster For a scene or a part from a play of its kind, by the better of the two. A brief’s steps speed up with material you’ve met before instead. 
    Its conventions. A Japanese film genre focused on the lives and dealings of the yakuza, Japan’s organized crime syndicates.
    What it asks of an actor. The 1960s ninkyo (chivalry) yakuza film formulaically climaxed in the hero’s katana showdown against a rival gang. The 1970s jitsuroku cycle replaced it with chaotic, unheroic brawls and gunplay. Sources describe the fights, not actors’ training.
    Why it adds no requirement. What the research found (Fights And Stunts) belongs to the lead’s climactic sword fight in ninkyo eiga, the chivalry yakuza films of the 1960s, not to the genre as a whole, so the genre adds no requirement of its own.
    What the research says: Tradition and Modernity in Japanese Yakuza Films of the 1960s and 70s: Ninkyo climaxes feature 'the hero armed only with a traditional katana sword slicing his way through a gang of bad yakuza with guns'; in Battles Without Honor and Humanity yakuza’s 'hands shake when they fire a gun'; 'a free-for-all brawl, with Tetsuya Watari fighting in a downpour'. Yakuza Films at the Asia Society: 1960s yakuza films were 'always climaxing in a showdown where the anti-hero takes down all nemeses with a katana', 'a bloody ballet of sword swinging'. 10 great Japanese gangster films: Ken Takakura as 'the stoic, tattooed, katana-wielding tough guy'; Fukasaku’s jitsuroku style 'stripped away the mythos and surface sheen of the old-school ninkyo eiga of the 1960s'. (all three of its sources read directly, reviewed October 10, 2026; low confidence).
    Still uncertain: The sources are film criticism about screen content, not about what actors trained for. A whole-genre combat demand might be argued from violence across cycles, but no source says it, and much later yakuza cinema (Kitano’s, for one) isn’t led by fight choreography.
    How its scenes are played. The ninkyo film’s chivalric hero against a rival gang, and the loyalties of the syndicate. This comes from the genre’s research, mostly held with low confidence.
    Command Of The Frame: Status and threat carried in the body and the look more than the words: the person everyone in the room watches. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and execution, and calls on your presence and physical expression. Rehearsing on your feet: status is found standing, in how little you need to move.
    Played Between Two: The scene lives between the two of you: chemistry, contrast, a part each that only works against the other. A self-tape in it is judged more on connection and responsiveness, and calls on your presence, listening and responsiveness and emotional depth. Rehearsing on your feet: a partnership is built in the exchange, played through until each part answers the other.
    To study it. Press Study It beside it under Genres on your Player Profile. You’ll watch a film of its kind. To tape its brief, press Open Its Brief beside it instead: the tape is judged beat by beat like any taped brief, on what its own material asks.

## The ways of playing a scene

  Pace: Measured: Taking the time each thought needs: the choices specific, the pauses earned.

  Pace: Driven: Picking up cues and pressing on: the scene played at the speed of what the characters want.

  Scale: Restrained: Less shown than felt: the feeling held under the surface.

  Scale: Expansive: Sized to the room: voice, body and feeling played out to the audience.

  Structure: Set: Fixed and repeatable: every beat placed, the same each time.

  Structure: Open: Live each time: taking the next moment from what the partner gives.

  bands: Suits The Scene, Either Way, Works Against The Scene

## A take’s account of how it was played

  suited: The way you played it suited the scene (Restrained, Suits The Scene) and added 1.1 points to the take (+1.5 to believability, −0.4 to connection). Apart from that, your abilities and techniques cost it 4.2 points.

  against: The way you played it worked against the scene (Expansive, Works Against The Scene) and cost the take 2.3 points (−1.5 to believability, −0.4 to connection). Apart from that, your abilities and techniques cost it 3 points.

  neither: The way you played it made no difference to the scene either way (Measured, Either Way). Apart from that, your abilities and techniques cost it 2.5 points.

  kept, suited: Played that way, it suited the scene (Restrained, Suits The Scene) and added 1.1 points. Your abilities and techniques cost it 4.2 points.

  kept, against: Played that way, it worked against the scene (Expansive, Works Against The Scene) and cost 2.3 points. Your abilities and techniques cost it 3 points.

  kept, neither: Played that way, it made no difference to the scene either way (Measured, Either Way). Your abilities and techniques cost it 2.5 points.
